# The Kingdom of Christ, Volume 1 (Third Edition): A Modern Paraphrase

*The Kingdom of Christ, Volume 1 (Third Edition), by Frederick Denison Maurice, restated paragraph by paragraph in modern English. This paraphrase follows the order and content of the original without adding interpretation. Drafted with claude-sonnet-5-5; reviewed by a human editor.*

# The Kingdom of Christ, Volume 1 (Third Edition)

*The passage:* Bishop Taylor, in *Via Intelligentiæ*, asks what is now to be done. Must truth stay in the dark forever, the world stay divided, societies be disturbed, governments weakened, and our spirits corrupted by error, uncertain opinions, and the pedantry of talkative men? Surely there is a cure, and the wise Governor of the world has not failed in so necessary a matter as to lead us into all truth. But that way has not yet been found, though he has told us all the ways of man and his imaginings toward Truth and Peace, and we see that they will not do. Amid the waters of contention and dispute, and the little artifices of divided schools, we can find no rest for the soles of our feet. We have tried every way but one, which is God's way. Having missed all the others, let us try this.

## ADVERTISEMENT.

[*Prefixed to the Second Edition*, 1842.]

[This]{.smallcaps} work first appeared in 1838 as Letters to a Member of the Society of Friends. A controversy then dividing the Quaker Society prompted it. My main aim was to ask whether accepting the spiritual principles the Quakers professed meant rejecting Christian ordinances, or whether the one necessarily implied the other. This was almost the same question as another. The Quakers had tried to set up a spiritual kingdom in the world. Did such a Kingdom not exist already, and were the ordinances not its expression?

Among many smaller but serious mistakes, I found one that had made my purpose hard to understand. The early Quakers said the Spiritual Kingdom was bounded by no nation. Yet the Quaker Society has existed only in England and America. I wanted to show my Quaker correspondent a spiritual body in which he could find a home when the sect no longer gave him one. So in every Letter I naturally referred to the English Church, and to her sacraments, ministers and forms of worship. Many therefore took me to be writing a defense of that Church. If so, they asked, how had I kept my promise to show the Quakers a universal Church, such as they had dreamed of?

I also found that members of my own communion read the book far more than Quakers did. Some friends suggested that a new edition should become a Treatise on the Church, with every reference to the sect removed. I could not take this advice. If I have suggested to a Churchman anything he could not find far better put in a hundred other books, I owe it to my comparing our own position with that of those who seem farthest from us. Besides, if I lost sight of the Quakers, I would give up one means of repairing my error. By following their line of thought we may learn how earnest men have come to feel that they need a Catholic Church, and not merely a National one.

Still, though I cannot stop connecting my hints with Quaker principles, I must almost entirely omit the particular controversy that led me to address them. It seems to be over, and the Society is more likely to die by slow decay than by sudden convulsion. For this reason, and for some private ones, the form of Letters no longer fits, so I have rewritten and reconstructed the book, hoping partly to remove the fault I have described. I have also tried to remove some of the grosser errors, whether I found them myself or others pointed them out. Enough remain, no doubt, and my additions may have brought in more. I hope the book will be taken as nothing but a collection of Hints, which, if they lead the reader to deeper thought and greater reverence, may soon make him far wiser than his teacher.

The Dialogue that follows explains how the subject of the whole book connects with the history of the Quaker body. Its form is imaginary, but I have often voiced the same sentiments to members of the Society. The Quaker's account of the trials, from within and without, that beset a thoughtful man who finds a sectarian position no longer tenable may, I believe, be called an "ower true tale." I should add that the Archbishop of Dublin has lately published a work called "The Kingdom of Christ Delineated." Out of respect for so distinguished an author, and for selfish reasons too, I would have changed my title once I knew it had been taken. But the description of my book is closely tied to its intention, and this edition fits the name more strictly than the former one did. So I must keep it.

## TO THE REV. DERWENT COLERIDGE, STANLEY GROVE, CHELSEA.

MY DEAR MR. COLERIDGE,

[In]{.smallcaps} a note to your volume on the Scriptural character of the English Church, you mentioned the first edition of these Hints. You meant to correct one of my many inaccuracies. That was a kind service, and you made it more welcome by approving my general design. I would have valued such recognition from anyone who had thought so seriously about the subject. I valued it more from you, because your work had qualities that might have made me fear you would be less tolerant of mine. Its calm, scholarly tone and careful English style contrast strongly with the crudeness and haste of my Letters to a Quaker. Yet I was delighted to find that neither you nor the accomplished Editor of Mr. Coleridge's works was kept by these defects from taking an interest in my thoughts. You both saw in them one of a thousand signs of the influence your father's writings are having on this generation.

Everyone who has felt this influence must want to acknowledge it, so you may well be surprised that in a book of some length I referred to it so seldom. Twenty years ago you might have put this down to a cowardly fear of being linked with an unpopular name. But when I wrote, no one could have had such a motive. The English parties that in Mr. Coleridge's lifetime differed only in how much they disliked him were now scrambling for a share of his opinions. The only danger of another reaction seemed to me to lie in his admirers' ambition to make him answerable for their own statements of his views, or for their inferences from them. I did not want to contribute to that. I never had the privilege of knowing him, so I had no means of correcting the impressions I took from his works. I was liable to great mistakes in interpreting them, and to the moral temptation of bending them to my own purposes. I thought it better to seem ungrateful and a plagiarist, even to myself, than to risk using his name to support sentiments he might have disapproved. I also did not want to keep anyone from profiting by his wisdom because I had taught them to connect it with my follies.

This caution did little good. The only two reviews that, so far as I know, paid my book any attention, one treating it with extreme kindness and the other with unbounded contempt, both put my name beside Mr. Coleridge's, flatteringly but undeservedly. I could not help fancying that one critic would gladly have had readers credit the master with the monstrous absurdity, self-sufficiency, love of priestcraft, hatred of the rights of conscience, and preference of Fathers and Councils to Scripture that were declared characteristic of the disciple. Anyone spoken of in this way should explain, as well as he can, how far the charge is true that he took his method of thought from his supposed teacher, and, if it is true, how far that teacher answers for his use of the method. I want to make that explanation now, to ease my own mind and to rescue your father's memory from any injury I may have done it. I might have made my confession to many dear friends who admire his writings. I choose to make it to one of his family, for two reasons. First, those who have profited most by what he taught do not and cannot form a school, and the English public, with its party notions, should not suppose they do. Second, my feeling toward him, though not based on personal acquaintance, is so strictly and vividly personal that I cannot bear to think of him chiefly as a writer of books, and I am always glad to connect him with a living representative.

Some people cannot feel affection for a book unless they can associate it with a living man. I am not sure I share that incapacity, but I admit that Mr. Coleridge's books interest me mainly as the biography of a man who passed through the struggles of the age we are succeeding, and who, after great effort and sorrow, found a resting-place. There are the juvenile poems showing him seeking in Unitarianism a refuge from the flatness and falsehood of a mere State Christianity. There is the fierce, magnificent ode in which he sees the old European world of convention and oppression falling apart and rejoices. There is the noble recantation of his hopes from republican ascendancy. And there is his ode to Despondency, which so perfectly gives the feelings of a man who, after all practical hopes failed, sought deliverance in meditation, and on returning to the actual world found its glory gone and his capacity for enjoyment dead. These poems seem to me so intensely and painfully real, and so expressive of what thousands of minds must have felt in different measures, that I doubt I have ever judged any of them merely as art. I do not think this conflicts with the belief that in him, as in every great poet, creative power implied self-forgetfulness and the ability to pass beyond personal experience. No one can utter the thoughts of others as well as his own, or be in any degree the spokesman of his time, without this quality. But I should imagine it nearly always goes with much inward suffering, since the person who enters most into what many others experience does, in the liveliest sense, experience it himself. I have no right to speak on these points, and if I speak ignorantly you must remember that I only report my own impressions, not a standard for other readers. Your father's greater poems, such as the Ancient Mariner and Christabel, belong undoubtedly to pure imagination. But I question whether I would be as interested even in these if I did not find many veins and fibres connecting them with his personal being, and if they did not help me read the history of his mind and so the history of our time.

Just as I have never learnt to separate his poetic genius from himself, I fear I cannot appreciate him formally and abstractly as a philosopher. In his "Friend" I seem to find the same man I had known amid the storms of the revolutionary period. He is no less impatient of mere rules and decrees than he was then, but the impatience has taken a new form. He has become convinced that society is a reality, and that unmaking and reconstructing it would not make it more real. So he asks what the grounds of its reality are, and how we may be kept from turning it into a fiction and a falsehood. I do not say this inquiry is complete and satisfactory. I am glad it is not, since I believe a more complete one would not have been half so profitable as this has been and will be for generations. Its merit is that it is an inquiry: it shows us what to seek and puts us in the way of seeking. That is why it has offended more than one class of persons. The mere Destructive complains that it recognizes the worth of what ought to be swept away. The mere Conservative is indignant that it will not take existing rules and opinions as an ultimate basis, but tries to find their meaning and foundation. The man of Compromises is most bitter, because it assumes the statesman has some law of conduct other than sailing with the wind. The mere Englishman is angry that taxes, libels, and the bombardment of Copenhagen are not treated as his favourite journals treat them. The man of Abstractions cannot see what such topics have to do with a scientific book. This combination of enemies, each able to call the book "*neither one thing nor the other*," is enough to explain any unpopularity it has met. Explaining its power despite these disadvantages, and others I need not hint at in writing to you, is harder. How has a book said to be utterly unpractical changed men's minds on the most practical subjects? How has a book said to have no sympathy with the spirit of this age affected the most thoughtful of our young men? I hope some Reviewer will one day undertake it. I am not trying to solve such problems, only to account for its influence on my own mind. That influence is owed mainly to the very peculiarities that seem to have lowered its worth in the opinion of wiser people. For this I am at least thankful: the book has not lessened my interest in others on the same subject, nor tempted me to set Mr. Coleridge up as the one teacher on it. It has enabled me to honour others, very different, from our own and earlier times, whom I might otherwise have misunderstood and, through ignorance and self-conceit, undervalued. Above all, it taught me to reverence the facts of history, and to believe that the least perversion of them to extract a moral is both a folly and a sin.

I have found similar help, in a different department of thought, in that still more irregular work, the *Biographia Literaria*. A young man today who is tormented by the puzzles of Society and the many systems for dissolving them is haunted almost as much by the problems of Criticism. He feels the connexion between his own life and the books he reads. He meets theories about that connexion, authoritative dogmas on the worth or worthlessness of particular poems and paintings, and paradoxical rebellions against those dogmas. He asks what authority antiquity has and what province belongs to our own time, tries to find permanent laws of art, and hears indignant assertions of its independence of all laws. Anyone who has watched himself or his contemporaries, or even read my rough list of these difficulties, will feel how tangled they are with our thoughts on the highest subjects. To say we need not understand ourselves on these questions, or that principles about them do not matter, is just to say we should not meddle with them. Someone never brought into contact with such topics need not think about them; but if he is, he will find that having no thoughts about them hinders him more than he expects in matters directly touching his personal life. Now if anyone reads Mr. Coleridge's literary life expecting a great poet to supply the principles of his art ready made, for immediate use and export, he will be disappointed. I cannot find a system here any more than in his political work. I have lately heard there is one, taken whole and alive from a great German author. I speak only of what I saw myself, and it escaped my notice. I seemed to see a writer feeling his way into questions that had puzzled me, and explaining his own progress out of the belief that all things depend on association, into the acknowledgment of something with which they are associated, and the discovery of a keynote to the harmony. From him, by practical illustration, I learnt how to enter into the spirit of a living or departed author without presuming to judge him, and how to learn what he means without imputing our meanings to him. I learnt that beauty is neither accidental nor artificial, but is to be sought as something in both nature and the mind of man, which by God's law binds us to nature. But all this comes out in a natural, experimental method, through tests and trials in which a man may be greatly helped by another's successes and failures, as Faraday may be helped by Davy. He cannot simply adopt them from another; we can adopt them from him only by catching his spirit of investigation and applying it to new facts.

The "Aids to Reflection" is a book of a different character, and I feel much deeper and more solemn obligations to it, though of the same kind. I want a politician or critic who has worked his own way through the region where he acts as my guide. I would be most dissatisfied with someone who wrote moral and spiritual aphorisms without showing he was himself in conflict with an evil nature and a reluctant will, and had received the truths he shares with me not at second hand but as the needed supports of his own being. I know no book that gave me clearer tokens of this than this one. Admirers and objectors alike describe it as dealing with religion philosophically. In whatever sense that is true, and in an important sense I believe it is, I can testify that it delivered me from many philosophical phrases and generalisations that attach themselves to the truths of the Creed, even in minds that think they receive Christianity with a childlike spirit. It helped me see that the deepest principles are ones the Peasant can grasp and enter into as well as the Schoolman. I value his philosophy mainly because it led me to this discovery, and to the practical conclusion that teachers must cultivate their understandings. They must do so in order to tell what is factitious and accidental, belonging to our artificial habits of thought, from what is fixed and eternal, belonging to man as man, which God will open the eyes of every humble man to see. In this way I learnt the preciousness of the simple Creeds of antiquity, and the inward witness that a gospel of facts has and a gospel of notions must lack. I learnt how the most awful and absolute truths, which notions displace or obscure, are bound up in facts. Through facts they can be held by those who cannot compare notions and have a blessed incapacity for resting in them.

The first person to apply this principle to religious questions will inevitably be caught sometimes in the obscurity he is trying to free us from. Whoever starts to challenge notions and theories will himself be counted the greatest notionalist and theorist. To clear away crudities and confusions he will sometimes have to adopt or invent a nomenclature. His strict adherence to it will be called pedantry. His followers will deserve the charge if they repeat his words instead of carrying their meaning into their studies and lives. His enemies will have a plausible pretext for saying he has made simple truths complex. The "Aids to Reflection" has suffered all these misfortunes. Yet I have heard it denounced as unintelligible by people I found very hard to understand, who kept perplexing me with hard words to which I could find nothing corresponding among actual things. They tried to explain mysteries by the very events that were most mysterious to me, and which, being nearest to me, I most needed to understand for my practical life. On the other hand, I have heard the simplest, most childlike men and women express an almost rapturous thankfulness for being allowed to read it, since it helped them understand their own hearts, their Bibles, and the connexion between them. I believe it has offended, and always will offend, many, not for its theories but for its essentially practical character. Its manly denunciation of the sentimental school must pain many today who have in practice adopted the Rousseau cant with slightly altered words. They praise men for being good though they do monstrously evil acts, and think it a vulgar worship of decency to say that one who is the slave of his own passions, and enslaves others to them, may not be a very right and true man after all. Yet those who really exalt decency above inward truth and conformity to a high standard will not own Mr. Coleridge as an ally just because the school that pretends to oppose them rejects him. The whole aim of his book is to draw us from a merely worldly and external morality to what concerns the heart and the inner man. Here again he is unfortunate, for those who have turned "heart-religion" into a phrase, and put the feelings and experiences of their minds in place of the laws those feelings might, rightly used, lead us to, will be sure to see him as peculiarly their enemy. If everyone in the land belonged to one of these classes, and many had not tried them all and grown weary of them all, it would be hard to understand how this volume has found its way into so many studies and so many hearts.

The idea of the first "Lay Sermon," that the Bible is the Statesman's Manual, is less developed than any of those I have mentioned. But the bare announcement of it has been worth more to me than any lengthy exposition I know. No topic in these volumes has engaged me more than the national history of the Bible, and I have said very little that did not have that thought as its germ.

The little book on Church and State you will suppose, from the title and character of these volumes, I have studied more attentively still. If you watch me closely, you will probably find many more thoughts I have stolen from it than I am aware of, though I am conscious of obligations in superabundance. The doctrine I have tried to bring out on the relations between Church and State seems to me nothing but an expansion of Mr. Coleridge's remark on the opposition and necessary harmony of Law and Religion. Here, as elsewhere, I have departed from his phraseology and even adopted one he might not sanction.

The robberies I have confessed are such in the truest sense: conscious and deliberate. If someone found twenty or thirty pages in my book traceable to some English or foreign author, common sense, even without charity, would make him hesitate to charge me with wilful fraud. It is far likelier that I mistook for my own something long mixed into my compositions than that I took such a stupid, blundering way of earning a reputation that a few years must destroy. A court of justice, on the mere evidence, would lean to the tolerant side. If it hesitated, it would be either because an insignificant author can do with impunity what a famous writer with enemies on every side would be mad to try, or because someone who had merely forgotten would have had a vague impression of reading the passage elsewhere. Being honest, he would then have dropped some hint, even a wrong one, of where it came from, making the reader curious to see what the other writer said. If I had done *that*, any competent judge who did not deliberately pervert his judgment would drop all suspicion of evil design. But my use of your father's writings is of an entirely different kind. It could not be proved by collating paragraphs, so if I wished to conceal it I would be really, not apparently, dishonest. And this holds even though the main subject of my book is one he has not, so far as I know, distinctly treated. The thoughts he scatters about it, though deeply interesting, do not always satisfy me, so I have commonly been without his guidance and have sometimes wilfully deserted it. I will not fulfil the purpose of this letter if I do not show how these two apparently opposite statements are reconciled.

No man will be of much use to his generation who does not give himself mainly to the questions occupying those who belong to it. An antiquary, I daresay, has an easier and quieter life than one who interferes with his contemporaries and joins their speculations. But his quiet is his reward, and those who seek another must give it up. Every man is often tempted to rest in some little nook of thought where others will not molest him because he does not molest them. Those with work assigned to them are soon driven by an irresistible power out of such retirement into the dusty highways of ordinary business and dispute. This, I think, was your father's peculiar merit and honour. The subjects he addressed were not those his poetical or metaphysical leanings would have chosen. They were exactly the questions of the time, those other men were discussing in the spirit of the time. As we of a younger generation have inherited these questions, we inherit also the wisdom that dealt with them. But there are questions we have not inherited, which belong more expressly to us than to our immediate predecessors. I suspect we must humbly study these for ourselves. It will make a great difference whether we invent a way of investigation or try to walk a path that better men have cleared of rubbish and marked with footprints and signposts.

One such question is the one your father was led, by the soundest wisdom I believe, largely to banish from his consideration after the French Revolution taught him the unspeakable importance of a distinct National life. I mean the question whether there is a Universal Society for man as man. I have given reasons in these volumes why everyone today must be more or less consciously occupied with this inquiry, and why no other topic, however important, can keep it from taking nearly the most prominent place in our minds. Another question, apparently from a different region of thought but touching this at more than one point, is how all thoughts, schemes, systems, and speculations may contribute their share to one that shall be larger and deeper than any of them. If I am indebted to your father on one account more than another, it is for showing me a way out of the dreadful vagueness and ambition such a scheme involves. He led me not merely to say but to feel that knowledge of The Being is the object we must strive after, and that all pursuit of Unity without this is the pursuit of a phantom. Yet I cannot help believing there is a right meaning hidden in this desire, that it will haunt us till we find it, and that we cannot merely denounce or resist it in ourselves or others. We shall do far more good, perhaps the very good we are meant to do in this age, if we steadily consider it. A further question thrusts itself on us continually and stirs more party feeling just now than any other. It concerns the doctrines expressed in the old Creeds about the nature of God himself. Are they to be taken on trust from the early ages, or treated as matters for our own inquiry, acknowledged only so far as they accord with what seems to us the declaration of Scripture or the verdict of reason? In preparing to consider this great subject I have felt, with many others, that Mr. Coleridge's help has been invaluable. Nearly every thoughtful writer of the day would have taught us that the highest truths lie beyond the limits of Experience, and that the essential principles of the Reason cannot be proved by syllogisms. The evidence for them is the impossibility of admitting what falls under the law of experience unless we recognize them as its foundation, and the impossibility of believing that we or anything else exist except on these terms. The atheism of Hume has driven men to these blessed discoveries. It was your father's honour to assert them to an age and nation that had not yet seen the need of them, but he did not pretend, and no one should pretend, that he was their first reviver or expositor. The application of these principles to *Theology*, however, I believe we owe mainly to him. He taught me that by the very law of the Reason the knowledge of God must be *given* to it, that the moment it tries to create its Maker it denies itself, and that the most opposite kind of Unity to the one Unitarianism dreams of is needed if the reason's demands are to be met. I must acknowledge I received this from him, or prove ungrateful to the highest Teacher, who might have chosen another instrument but has been pleased, in many cases I know of, to use this one. This instruction seems to me a most precious preparation for the inquiry that belongs more strictly to our age, but only a preparation. Reading the profound and, to a theological student, invaluable hints on the Trinity in Mr. Coleridge's writings, I cannot help feeling: "This is not enough." If reason is, as he said, expressly the human faculty, belonging to rich and poor alike, then not only the truths belonging to each person's condition but this highest truth, which he presents as demanding the highest efforts of thought and abstraction, must belong to the humblest man. It must be a sacred part of his inheritance, and must somehow be capable of being presented to him. Anyone who entertains this thought will find this theological subject soon tangled with the other two. The hope that some day

> "Wisdom may teach her lore\
> In the low huts of them that toil and groan,"

grows much brighter if we can really believe the deepest lore is the most universal. The hope that diverse sides of thought may be reconciled can begin to part from the dreary vision of a comprehensive System from which all life is excluded, if the central Unity is that of the living Being.

But how can such a dream ever be realized? To me the promise of it came in sounds that belong to our nursery, in the words by which our infants are baptized. Here it seemed to me lay the assurance that this truth belongs to no esoteric region. It is one of those all-embracing mysteries that surround us at every moment and gradually draw us into themselves, and it becomes most truly ours when we gain most of the privilege of men by becoming most like little children. So I was led to consider Baptism as a key to the nature of ordinances generally. I found they had been much prized by Luther and by the most earnest of those who, like him, regarded Christianity almost solely in its reference to their own personal life. They felt the extreme danger of substituting their belief for its object, and so destroying the reality of both. Their testimony was of the highest practical value, and the experience of those who had rejected ordinances to reach a more spiritual state of mind abundantly confirmed it to me. Still I could not see how someone starting from their point of view could ultimately escape the conclusion so generally adopted by the Reformers' disciples: that he who first reveres inward Truth and then comes to revere outward Signs begins in the spirit and is made perfect in the flesh. I could entirely sympathize with Mr. Coleridge's feeling that those who exalt Ordinances by turning them into Charms are not harmlessly adding to what was sufficient before, but destroying its meaning and reality. But suppose they are signs to the Race, signs of the existence of that universal body we were inquiring after. Then they take on a very different importance. They become indispensable in a higher sense than their champions dream, who seem to value them chiefly as means of exclusion. They are the very voice in which God speaks to his creatures, the witness that their fellowship with each other rests on their fellowship with him and both on the mystery of his Being, and the means by which we are meant to rise to the highest blessings he has given us. So there rose before me the idea of a [Church Universal]{.smallcaps}, built not on human inventions or human faith but on the very nature of God and the union he has formed with his creatures. It is a Church revealed to man as a fixed and eternal reality by means that infinite wisdom itself devised. Its tokens and witnesses, it seemed to me, must be Divine. But the feeling of its necessity, and apprehensions of its different sides and aspects, must, if it is a reality, be found in all the schemes that express human thought and feeling. No amalgamation of these can create a real harmony, but each may find its highest meaning in the harmony God has created, of which he is himself the centre.

These are the leading thoughts I have tried to express in this book. So you will understand me when I say I may have uttered innumerable sentiments for which your father would not have chosen to be responsible, even while I wished to study and apply his lessons. I conclude he would not have agreed with my views on Baptism. He would probably have thought I over-exalted the Ministry, would not have accepted every statement of mine on the Eucharist, and would have judged me wrong in some opinions about the Scriptures. On all these subjects I have deviated from what I think would have been his judgment, without losing any reverence and affection for his memory, and perhaps without coming any nearer than he did to the sentiments of any party dividing the Church. I am sure I would not have had courage to differ from them or him if he had not helped me believe that Truth is above both, most of all above myself and my petty notions, that it is worth seeking and loving above all things, and that He who is Truth is ready, if we obey him, to guide us into it.

I have been so occupied with a subject I am sure interests you above all others that I have left myself no time to express as I would wish my gratitude for your personal kindness and for the benefit of my opportunities of intercourse with you. But I cannot conclude without wishing you God-speed in the noble undertaking you are engaged in. If you are permitted to raise up a body of wise and thoughtful teachers out of our trading classes, you will do more for the Church than all those who write treatises about it. The trials and discouragements will be in proportion to the importance and novelty of the work. In these I trust you will be sustained by the highest consolations a Christian man and priest can know. But there will be times when you need lower helps too, if they are of the right kind. I can think of few more cheering than the recollection that you are carrying into effect principles your father urged on our countrymen years ago. You are doing what you can to prove that one who was called a theorist and a dreamer was in truth labouring to bring the most practical benefits to his country and to mankind.

Believe me,
My dear Mr. Coleridge,
Yours very sincerely,
F. MAURICE.

## INTRODUCTORY DIALOGUE WITH A QUAKER.

*Quaker.* Are you serious in saying you do not want me to leave the Society I was brought up in?

*Author.* I did not say that. I hope you will not abandon the *principles* you were brought up in.

*Q.* You mean the general morality and common Christianity that you and I were both taught to revere?

*A.* No. I do not believe you will keep those general principles and common doctrines unless you also keep the ones that belong to Quakerism.

*Q.* What can that mean except that I should stay in the Society?

*A.* I can answer better if you tell me what now tempts you to leave it.

*Q.* It is a long story, and I fear you will not hear it out. I was first startled by a contradiction between what our members profess and what they do.

*A.* You may change many times before you find a body where you will not notice the same thing.

*Q.* I know that. I hope I have enough sense not to give up a serious conviction because hypocrites pretend to it or a good man holds it weakly. That was not my difficulty. The contradiction seemed to run through all our arrangements, and even, to some degree, through the constitution of our Society. We are witnesses for spirituality, and I feel we are rank formalists.

*A.* Corruption may have crept into your system, but why judge it by its present appearance? Is reform impossible?

*Q.* For a long time I thought it possible. I decided to study the early Quakers, where I expected to find the higher, purer spirit that seems to have left us.

*A.* Were you disappointed?

*Q.* I thought not. Those writers showed deep earnestness, courage, self-denial, an intense conviction that what they said was true, and a readiness to live and die that others might hear it.

*A.* So you concluded that your Society had a sound foundation?

*Q.* If I had been convinced of that, I do not think I would have despaired of its present state. But here was my perplexity. I had read the Scriptures diligently and knew something of my own heart, and what our Quaker ministers said about human depravity, the grace of God, and redemption by Christ seemed to me mostly vague and unsatisfactory. Still, the doctrines on these subjects that are commonly, and I think rightly, called Evangelical are recognised among us, and some ministers bring them out prominently. The older Quakers, whom I was so inclined to admire on other grounds, show these doctrines far less distinctly. They seem to blame other Christians for attaching importance to them, and sometimes use expressions almost incompatible with believing them.

*A.* Did you think they put notions of their own in place of these doctrines?

*Q.* I reluctantly came to suspect so. That is why I wonder that you talk of my keeping the principles of Quakerism. They seem to me opposed to what I suppose you consider the leading tenets of the Gospel.

*A.* How did you come to that persuasion?

*Q.* By the workings of my own mind, and then it was confirmed by two people who differed in many of their views.

*A.* Who were they?

*Q.* The first was an Independent dissenter. He strongly admired the language and conduct of Fox and Penn on many occasions. He said he could not join them in objecting to all payment of ministers, or in disliking baptism, and several such points. But he wished that the energy they showed in denouncing ecclesiastical superstitions and oppressions might pass to English dissenters.

*A.* That was surely very complimentary.

*Q.* But when he came to Fox's theology his tone changed completely. He called Fox a mystic, ignorant of the truths of the Gospel, who proclaimed theories that utterly subverted them. A clergyman of your Church who was present held the same opinion, if anything more earnestly. However much he disliked Fox's indecorous and violent language about the Established Church, he thought Fox's doctrine of an Inward Light immeasurably more dangerous. It was, he said, utterly incompatible with the Bible and with the experience of every true Christian.

*A.* Perhaps you remember some of the arguments your two friends used?

*Q.* Their arguments did not impress me as much as the clear, settled persuasion they shared, that if Fox were right, the Bible must be wrong. You may smile that I gave weight to assertions when I cannot recall the evidence for them, but I know that assertions coming from deep conviction often move me more than elaborate logical proofs.

*A.* It is the same with me, so I will not ridicule your admission.

*Q.* I am glad, because ridicule does not generally do me good. After these two gentlemen had impressed me strongly with their serious objections, they thought it fitting to indulge their humour at the expense of our founders and their mystical notions.

*A.* Having entered the castle, they were naturally anxious to dismantle it.

*Q.* They took away more of my defences than they perhaps meant to. While they were with me I felt bound to say something for feelings and thoughts I had held sacred from childhood, even if they were deserting me. When I was alone I found I had defended my Quakerism poorly. But I had also seen cracks and hollows in their system that I had not noticed before. Their wit shook my reverence both for my own faith and for what I had hoped would replace it.

*A.* A melancholy state of mind, as all who have known it can testify.

*Q.* In that state I read two Unitarian books, one by an Englishman of the last century and one by an American of the present.

*A.* The former at least did not treat your mystic feelings more kindly than your evangelical friends had.

*Q.* No. But he taught me that the Calvinistic doctrine could be ridiculed just as well as they and he ridiculed mine. I could not judge the value of his critical remarks and discoveries. But he said enough to convince me that Scripture has often been strained to support the opinions he attacked, and that the positive texts in their favour were far fewer than I had imagined. His views of God's character and his feelings toward his fellow-creatures also agreed better with my conscience than what I had heard from the members of any sect but my own.

*A.* So the impression he left was on the whole pleasing?

*Q.* By no means. When I put the book down I felt utter coldness and dreariness. The very idea of a spiritual world and a spiritual life seemed missing from it. Materialism was the point all its speculations were heading toward, if it was not their basis.

*A.* I should think that was not the case with the American.

*Q.* No, he was of a very different temper. Some of his statements were not unlike things I had met in Fox and Penn. His mind was more comprehensive than theirs, more able to take interest in ordinary affairs and general literature, yet scarcely less morally exalted, and at times I was inclined to say scarcely less spiritual.

*A.* Could you comfortably accept his religious scheme?

*Q.* I almost fancied I could. There was something so capacious and engaging in it. But just as I finished the book I fell ill, and before I recovered I heard that one of my oldest and dearest friends had died. Then my interest in both the Englishman and the American vanished. I think I disliked the American most, because his promises were the fairest.

*A.* But did you not say you had traced a fine vein of humanity and spirituality in him?

*Q.* I thought so. But however humane or spiritual he was, he was not personal. There was nothing in him from which a soul struggling with life and death could draw the least help. He was plainly meant for sunshine and gala days. Then I remembered what my evangelical friends had said and the doctrines they had set before me. In that moment these seemed all-important, and I bitterly accused myself for thinking them narrow and hard. What had I to do with large views of men's happiness or God's character? My own soul was at war, and the friend I wanted was whoever could show it a way of peace. It seemed I had no right to trouble myself with anything else.

*A.* When you recovered, you probably sought the advice of the Independent dissenter you mentioned?

*Q.* For some reasons I wanted rather another interview with the clergyman who had spoken so strongly against Fox and mysticism, but just then he left the neighbourhood. I heard that his successor in the parish was an author, so I bought his books before venturing to call on him.

*A.* Did they encourage you to expect help from him?

*Q.* I thought I had been shaken enough already by my own discoveries and my various counsellors. Apparently not: these books were to upset me altogether.

*A.* How? Did you meet a sceptic disguised as a clergyman?

*Q.* Oh no, he was the most vehement enemy of all scepticism. He said that doctrines had been delivered in the earliest ages of Christianity which we should receive with simple, uninquiring faith. The blessings of salvation, he said, are tied to our submission to a certain system ordained by God. Evangelical teaching had almost entirely forgotten this system, or treated it as merely a matter of external arrangement. That teaching spoke of a period of conversion in which people pass from death to life, whereas the Church and Scripture attribute this wonderful change to baptism.

*A.* You did not believe these statements. Why did they affect you so powerfully?

*Q.* Partly, perhaps, because they were uttered with that strong, deep conviction which I admit always acts most strongly on me, especially when, as in this clergyman's case, it is backed by a laborious and self-denying life. But I think there was another cause. A person who has suffered severely from religious struggles has an inward longing for rest that no one else can know. Bruised, beaten, humbled, he cannot help listening to anyone who tells him he has been all wrong. He has been so tormented by his own miserable experiments and failures that he must be glad to hear he should give them up entirely. So after many struggles with my pride and my modesty, the shame of uttering my feelings and the pain of hiding them, I decided to state my difficulties to the new clergyman.

*A.* He can scarcely have expected a convert from such a quarter.

*Q.* He evidently did not want one. I was used to people who spared no pains to bring me over to their opinions, so the change was rather agreeable. But he also clearly did not understand me, and, if I am not mistaken, thought it would be wrong to understand me. I daresay I stammered and spoke incoherently when I tried to tell him my thoughts. One cannot speak as clearly about oneself as about the weather and the crops. And he made so many efforts to turn the talk to those subjects, and fixed such steady, clear, cold eyes on me when I asked for information on anything else, that I was no less embarrassed at the end of our interview than at the beginning.

*A.* How did he show that he misunderstood you?

*Q.* You know I was educated to look on all forms and ordinances as sinful. Those thoughts grew with my growth and strengthened with my strength. But lately I have repeatedly felt that these forms might be desirable, and even that something in me needs them. This was the point I especially wished to raise with Mr. ——. I knew that there were things it might not be right for him to teach me yet. But I thought that someone who considered forms most important to our moral life might have removed some of the difficulties clouding my mind about them, or at least interpreted some of the desires and longings I had conceived for them in spite of my prejudices. He seemed to think this wish irreverent and presumptuous. "I can enter into no explanations with you," he said; "you are not in a state to understand explanations. You have one plain duty: submit to the ordinances of Christ's Church, confess the sins of your fathers in withdrawing from it and your own in continuing the schism, receive Christian Baptism, and then I shall be happy to instruct you." "I am much inclined to take this course," I replied, "but I feel difficulties and scruples I do not know how to overcome." "Sir," he said, "the case may be stated very shortly. The Fathers of the Church, the Saints and Martyrs of the first five centuries, the Greek Church, the Romish Church, the English Church, all agree that Baptism is the one door by which we can enter Christ's fold, and so everlasting life. An ignorant mechanic in the seventeenth century said there was some other door, and you have chosen to disbelieve the whole Catholic Church and follow the mechanic. Is this a safe proceeding? If a speculation in cloth or wool were proposed to you, and a hundred competent judges said it would ruin you, while one person wholly unacquainted with the trade said it might turn out well, would you not think yourself mad to venture on it? Will you put your chances of salvation on a still greater risk?" With these words he wished me good morning.

*A.* How did this argument affect you?

*Q.* You may wonder, and I wonder myself, since it seems so consistent and plausible, but I never felt less moved by any words in my life. No, that is wrong: they did affect me. They almost counterbalanced the impression I had earlier formed in favour of your Church and its ordinances.

*A.* How could that be?

*Q.* Reverence was what I looked for above all in this clergyman. He had spoken of the sin of irreverence in his book, and my heart felt the truth of his words. He said it was his reverence that kept him from speaking to me about his faith. And yet he could teach me to calculate about eternal life as if it were merchandise! He spoke not to the part of me that was looking above the earth, but to my earthly, selfish nature. He did not speak to the part ready to submit to anything and bear anything to do God's will and know Him, but to all my proud, contentious, disputatious feelings. He did not address me as a creature capable of reverence, though he accused me of irreverence. He taught me to connect a lower, more grovelling notion of Christianity with it than I had ever held under the most irreverent of my teachers.

*A.* You felt, then, that you would be more right to remain a Quaker?

*Q.* No, I did not feel that. My mind was in the most wretched contradiction. But I said to myself: I am urged to forsake the body in which I and my fathers have lived. I am to turn away from those who first taught me to revere the workings of my own spirit and to tremble at the name of God, and to break all the bonds of old affection and sympathy. And why? Because higher objects and interests demand even this sacrifice? Because I must leave father and mother for the sake of Christ? No, because it is the safer, more politic course, because on a balance of probabilities I shall be less likely to suffer selfish loss here or hereafter. Magnify the loss as you will, and heap up the words infinite and eternal: this is a question of principle, not of degree, and when I set principle at nought, I believe those great and terrible words lose all meaning for me.

*A.* You must not unfairly suppose that the clergyman you spoke with attached no higher, more spiritual idea to those words than what he seems to have conveyed to you. He probably meant an *argument aimed at the man himself*.

*Q.* I understood that. I formed the conviction from parts of his book, and still hold it, that he has a very high and spiritual apprehension of things. I knew he would think me unfit to be admitted to his deeper mysteries, and I claimed no such honour. But from the little I have read about the catechumens in the primitive Church, I fancied they were not taught something wholly different in kind from what they learnt after baptism.[^fn022] I did not know they were reasoned with on the selfish motives which their later initiation must have aimed to cure. But be that as it may, an argument aimed at the man himself is meant, I suppose, to produce some kind of conviction, or else it is a gratuitous insult. I cannot imagine anyone on whom this kind of argument would produce conviction. It assumes that the person appealed to cares for nothing but profit and loss. Someone who cares for nothing else would merely smile at an attempt to rouse his fears about an unseen, future blessing, since his whole mind is wrapped up in what is passing around him. Your wisdom, therefore, consists in ignoring the part of him that might listen, and addressing the part that has no ears.

[^fn022]: See Note A.

*A.* But if this is an error, it is not peculiar to one school among us. In a commercial country we are all more or less inclined to act, think, and argue on such maxims.

*Q.* I do not think the clergyman would thank you for saying he took his notions from a commercial age. But I was not bringing a charge against a school. I was only telling you my own story.

*A.* It is a sad and eventful one. But you have not finished it?

*Q.* The next incident will surprise you most. I have scarcely the courage to confess that during the last two months I have had frequent and not unpleasant interviews with a Romish priest.

*A.* The argument from safety is so established and habitual among members of his Church that I think he cannot have been more agreeable to you than the English clergyman.

*Q.* He has rarely, if ever, used it. Perhaps he guessed from something I said at our first meeting that it would carry little weight with me. Far from denouncing my former thoughts and opinions, he has taken pains to show me how many points of sympathy there are between us.

*A.* Sympathy between a Romanist and a Quaker!

*Q.* "I find," he says, "in the writings of the early Quakers the most earnest longings after a universal Church. Is it wonderful that they felt such longings? What is called the English Church, the only one they knew, is limited to a particular locality, subjected to the yoke of a national sovereign, tied down by national laws and customs. Depend upon it, my friend, your founders were right: that is not Christ's Church which is not universal."

*A.* How did you answer that?

*Q.* I said our Quaker system was by its nature spiritual, and his Church seemed to me opposed to spirituality. "Opposed to spirituality!" he exclaimed. "Where will you find so many men and women of high spirituality, so many who resemble your own in their love of mystical contemplation, as among us? Has not Thomas à Kempis always been a favourite with your friends? And is not the 'Imitation of Christ' *the* book of Catholic devotion?" "These," I said, "were individuals, but what of the system?" "Well," he continued, "look at the system. What is your great complaint against the English system? Is it not that the mouths of men are shut who are urged by the Spirit to speak God's word? Can you bring that charge against us? Look at our friars, taken from the humblest classes, recognised the moment they show a real inward vocation, adopted at once as our teachers though they have no worldly qualification to recommend them. Do you not fault your English authorities because they forbid one whole sex to act as the handmaids of Christ and His flock? It is true we do not adopt your notion that women may speak in the churches, since we adhere more strictly to the words of Scripture. But the feeling that has led you to raise your voices for their rights and duties meets with every encouragement among us Catholics. We rejoice to see women devoting themselves to the Church's service, we give them all help and honour while they live, and we account them saints when they die." He found another point of agreement. "What is the great moving spring and centre of action to which all your writers refer? Surely love. They believe that though faith and hope are great Christian graces, the greatest of all is charity. That is the very principle we contend for. The Protestants wish to substitute faith for love. We say, as your friends also have said, that we will not."

*A.* Have these arguments brought your mind to greater quietness and satisfaction?

*Q.* Quietness and satisfaction! The words sound as if spoken in a dream. No indeed. I am as far from them as any poor mortal ever was. If the thought of joining the Romish Church ever presents itself, it comes as the fearful dream of something I may be driven to, a last hopeless alternative. Just as often it seems I may become a St. Simonian or a Socialist. These systems too have their attractions. They speak to wants in me which I think must be satisfied, and yet perhaps never are to be. Often I wish above all for a potion that would put me to sleep.

*A.* To sleep, my friend, perchance to *dream*.

*Q.* I know it well. This broken fever-sleep is worse than being awake.

*A.* Need I now answer the question you asked at the start of our conversation, what I meant by wishing you to keep your Quaker principles though you might leave the Quaker Society?

*Q.* Indeed you must. I am as much in the dark about the possibility of such a distinction as ever.

*A.* Your first objection to Quakerism arose from the feeling that it was not acting out its original idea.

*Q.* And my second from the feeling that the idea itself was false.

*A.* Let us consider. Did you really discover that idea to be false, or some other idea to be true?

*Q.* I found, or thought I found, certain great doctrines laid down in Scripture, most important to my own being. The early Quakers seemed to take only passing notice of them, if they did not reject them altogether.

*A.* I do not doubt that you found these doctrines in Scripture, that you found them practically important, or that the early Quakers comparatively neglected them. What I doubt is whether you ascertained that the doctrines the Quakers *did* notice were unimportant, unscriptural, or inconsistent with the others.

*Q.* I certainly thought they were.

*A.* Yes, and to that judgment of yours, formed perhaps on very hasty and insufficient evidence, you attached the same sacredness as to the witness of your heart and conscience, and of Scripture with them, that the doctrines your evangelical friends spoke of were needful to you.

*Q.* Is that a practical distinction?

*A.* You yourself recognised it most emphatically. Did you not say that while the dissenting minister and the evangelical clergyman were maintaining their own positions, they made the deepest impression on you? And that when they began to ridicule mystical opinions, they created the most vehement reaction in your mind against what you had been inclined to adopt?

*Q.* I certainly said so.

*A.* Well, in that confession I think I can find an explanation of all your later experience. You parted too suddenly with something God meant you to keep, and all the bewilderment and restlessness you have since suffered has been the necessary, appointed punishment of that error.

*Q.* If so, it is an error I cannot retrieve. The Evangelical, the Unitarian, the English clergyman, the Romanist may have left me nothing to fill the void in my mind, but they have effectively despoiled me of what was there before. They have not convinced me there is a standing-place in any of their systems, but they have made me certain I have none in my own.

*A.* Alas, it is thus that men, benevolent, honest, holy men, trifle with what is most awful and sacred in their brethren's minds! But they suffer as much evil as they inflict.

*Q.* In what way?

*A.* They turn the truths they hold in their inmost hearts, which God gave them to defend, into negations and contradictions. They are forced to resort to insufficient proofs and false assumptions in support of those truths, because they wilfully rejected the evidence God was supplying in the wants and cravings of their fellow men. They encourage infidelity under the name of faith. They form parties when they mean to proclaim principles that would make parties impossible. They set up theories and systems built on private judgments and individual conceits, while professing to lead us to permanent truths that belong to all and are necessary for all. They create new divisions by their very efforts to promote unity. They invent lines and landmarks of their own, while the great everlasting distinctions God has established escape them altogether.

*Q.* But why declaim against an evil so deeply rooted in human nature that six thousand years of effort to eradicate it have failed?

*A.* Why, indeed, if I did not believe that God has provided a complete and effectual witness against this evil, which is, as you say, so rooted in our selfish natures. I would not speak if I did not see that this witness has made itself heard in every age above all the clamours and distractions seeking to drown it. I would not speak if I were not convinced that the world would have been torn in pieces by its individual factions without this bond of peace and fellowship in its midst, or if I were not sure that peace is meant to drive out war, good evil, and light darkness. I would not speak if I could not see more abundant proofs of this glorious fact in our own day than in any before, or if it did not seem to me that all sects and factions, religious, political, or philosophical, bear testimony, sometimes mute, sometimes noisy, occasionally hopeful, oftener reluctant, to the presence of that Church Universal which is at once to justify their truths, explain the causes of their opposition, and destroy their existence.

*Q.* But the difficulty is that these factions have been in the Church Universal itself, whatever meaning you give that phrase.

*A.* That is just what I was asserting. There is a sect spirit, which labours to set up individual whims, opinions, and judgments in each of us: in us of the English Church as well as in the Romanist, the foreign Protestant, the Quaker, the Evangelical Dissenter, or the Unitarian. The question is whether we do not all, in our hearts and consciences, feel and know that this sect spirit is a vile, accursed, devilish spirit. If we know this, do not these same hearts and consciences testify that it is not meant to rule the world? And if that testimony is true, are we not bound to inquire what is to rule the world instead?

*Q.* And you think I may even yet enter on that inquiry with some hope?

*A.* The early Quakers testified that there was a [Kingdom of Christ]{.smallcaps} in the world, and that it would subdue all kingdoms to itself. Are you willing to inquire with me into their grounds for this assertion, to consider whether those grounds are tenable, and whether the Quaker system is or is not the realisation of the Quaker idea? Shall we then inquire into the principles of the religious bodies that want you to reject Quakerism, asking whether these also may be sound and true, whether they have been depraved and degraded by certain negative notions attached to them, and whether the systems invented to express them really do express them? If our conclusions on this last point are unsatisfactory, shall we then consider the Romanist's assertion that there is a Catholic Church which existed before all these systems and derives from a higher authority than any of them? If he can make that good, we may inquire whether the Romish system is this Church or its disease. We may ask whether that system has exalted the ordinances of the Church which its supporters acknowledge and revere, or degraded them and deprived them of their significance. And we may ask whether this Church Catholic contradicts the ideas held by the Quakers and other Protestant bodies, or is their legitimate and perfect realisation. We cannot finish this investigation without examining the point on which your Romanist friend found so close a resemblance between your views and his: whether a national society and a universal society are by nature contradictory and incompatible, or have been made so only by notions that interfere both with the universality of the spiritual body and with the distinctness of the national body. When we have reached some conclusion on this, we shall be able to speak of our position in England. We can ask whether there is a Catholic Church here or not, and if there is, under what circumstances it exists and what its dangers and evils are. We can ask whether those dangers and evils are reasons for living in separation from it or for uniting ourselves more closely to it.

*Q.* I am ready to hear what you have to say on these subjects, though I cannot pretend to look for any great discoveries.

*A.* I rejoice that you do not. If we begin by expecting great results, our pride will be rewarded with disappointment, and we shall add one more scheme to those that looked so fair and proved so abortive. If we desire to walk humbly along the path God has marked out for us, rejecting no light however feeble that He vouchsafes, and trusting Him to guide us to the perfect day, I do not think His promise to wayfaring men will go unfulfilled for us.

## PART I. *ON THE PRINCIPLES OF THE QUAKERS AND OF THE OTHER RELIGIOUS BODIES WHICH HAVE ARISEN SINCE THE REFORMATION, AND ON THE SYSTEMS TO WHICH THEY HAVE GIVEN BIRTH*.

### CHAPTER I. QUAKERISM.

#### Section I.—On the Positive Doctrines of the Quakers.

::: {.center}
The Indwelling Word—The Spiritual Kingdom—Spiritual Influences.
:::

[Mr]{.smallcaps}. Gurney, in his work on the peculiarities of the Society of Friends, says that the doctrine "which lies at the root of all their particular views and practices, is that of the perceptible influence and guidance of the Spirit of truth." Earlier he maintains that "a measure of the influence of the Spirit is bestowed upon all men whereby they are enlightened, and *may* be saved." Clearly he does not think this second principle nearly as important or as characteristic of the Quakers as the first. I can see why. A mere theory about the condition of the world cannot matter to a man as much as a principle that touches his own conduct and responsibility. But I doubt that the old Quakers would have stated the second doctrine in Mr. Gurney's terms. I think they would have given it a more practical form, and in doing so would have shown the relative place and value of these two parts of their creed quite differently.

Anyone who reads Fox's Journal will find that he held to perceptible impressions and influences in the most literal and practical way. His whole conduct was governed by the conviction that he was commanded to do certain acts and say certain words. Wherever he went, whomever he denounced, whatever tone he took, he believed he was obeying a divine prompting. The belief may seem strange today, and some of its results would seem strange even to Quakers. Yet no one who knows the years between 1645 and 1660 will think Fox and his followers were unusual in this. Hundreds, perhaps thousands, in Cromwell's army lived and acted under the same belief, and as consistently as any Quaker. Fox often clashed with such men. Again and again he speaks of the Ranters who troubled him, on this very ground that they all believed themselves under perceptible spiritual influences. In one remarkable passage he reports that his convert Justice Hotham told him he had been raised up to utter a principle that defeated these Ranters, and that without it they would have overrun and destroyed the whole land.

I\. This principle, then, and not the doctrine of perceptible influences, must have been the central one of Primitive Quakerism. An earnest Quaker would even have let the truth and value of his spiritual impressions be tested by their agreement or disagreement with it. James Naylor, whose strange doings at Bristol are in our ordinary histories, confessed he had been deceived by a false spirit or the fleshly workings of his own mind. Yet he professed his faith in Fox's principle to the last, and saw his errors as the result of departing from it. What was the principle? William Penn, in his preface to Fox's Journal, puts it this way: "They were directed to the light of Jesus Christ within them as the seed and leaven of the kingdom of God; near all, because in all, and God's talent to all. A faithful and true witness and just monitor in every bosom, the gift and grace of God to life and salvation, that appears to all, though few regard it." He calls this "their fundamental principle, the corner-stone of their fabric," their "main distinguishing point," and says the light of Christ within, given for man's salvation, "is the root of the goodly tree of doctrines, that grew and branched out of it."

Every page of Fox's Diary shows that this doctrine was the ground of his teaching. A conviction that the Spirit led him might make him break out in this or that steeple-house, or attack this or that Independent, Baptist, Presbyterian or "Common-Prayer man." But when he spoke, his words were: "Brother, there is a light within thee: resist it and thou art miserable; follow it and thou art happy." He says repeatedly that these words had a real moral effect on his hearers, and that everything else he said was valuable only as it arose from them or illustrated them. He believed he spoke to something already in those he addressed, and that, being there, it answered his appeal.

Fox did not learn this from the teachers or popular books of his day. The way he describes his early life is quite unlike Puritan biographies. "At eleven years of age," he says, "he knew pureness and righteousness"; as a child he was taught how to walk so as to be kept pure. Apprenticed to a shoemaker who dealt in wool and cattle, with a great deal passing through his hands, he never wronged anyone, "for the Lord's power was over him to preserve him," and people generally loved him for his honesty and innocence. His later inward conflicts had nothing to do with the religious or political controversies tearing England apart. Of Prelacy or Covenant, King or Parliament, he knew nothing. The question that filled his soul was: What am I, and what have I to do in this strange confused world? Every man must meet that question afresh, though thousands have been vexed by it before. Those Fox consulted gave him little help, so he withdrew from people and studied his Bible. Even that did not seem to tell him the secret. But he learnt one thing: something in him shrank from the inquiry and wanted to forget it, and something else in him would have no rest until it found the answer. Was this not already a great discovery, showing him in part what kind of being he was? He had desires that drew him down to what he could see, taste and handle, and desires that aspired to something his senses and appetites had nothing to do with. There was a second discovery in this. Real earthly objects attracted him, and his nature inclined to them, yet when he obeyed that nature he seemed to lose what was most real in himself. Must there not be a counter-attraction, as real as any of those things, raising him out of them and urging him toward a real, substantial good? Must that power not be in truth greater than all that resisted it, however things might seem? Could he not obey it, and by obeying find life and peace? He felt that he could, and was meant to. The light was stronger than the darkness, and he was privileged to dwell in it.

But was this light given only to George Fox the shoemaker? How could that be? It witnessed to him that whenever he was setting himself up, he was resisting it; when he followed his selfish inclinations, he knew he was fleeing this great teacher; and when he wished to be led by it, he knew he was a man. So it must be given to him because he was a man, and must be "a light which lighteneth every man who cometh into the world." A terrible majority might strive against it, but their striving proclaimed the truth, since the kind of misery men experienced showed the happiness intended for them.

Once he reached this conviction, the Bible seemed a new book to him. From first to last it witnessed to the invisible good men are to seek and against the visible idolatries that draw them from it. The lives of the patriarchs, Moses and the prophets were lives of men following the light, the teacher of their hearts, the Lord of righteousness, and resisting the appetites that would make them slaves and worshippers of outward things. The records of the sins of the Jews and of the heathen were records of revolts from this guide, by men who chose darkness rather than light, the outward and apparent good rather than the inward and real. The darkness grew grosser in each person who gave himself up to it, and the light brighter in each who steadily followed it. So it was in each new period: greater blindness and sensuality on one side, greater and more immediate illumination on the other. Jews and Gentiles grew more estranged from the One who was revealing Himself to both, while holy prophets held more wonderful converse than their fathers with the Word of God, rose further above outward emblems and institutions, and obeyed his inward suggestions more fully. This, or nearly this, is how the Old Testament seems to have appeared to Fox. So the opening of St. John's Gospel seemed to him to fit naturally with all the records it refers to. And St. Paul's declarations in the first and second chapters of Romans were no puzzle needing critical ingenuity. Paul says the Gentiles knew God but did not glorify Him or care to keep Him in their knowledge. He says Gentiles as well as Jews who seek glory, honour and immortality will obtain eternal life, and Jews as well as Gentiles who are contentious and obey unrighteousness will have tribulation and wrath. To Fox this was the plain announcement of a truth with which the rest of Scripture agreed.

II\. But how was men's condition affected by the appearance of our Lord in human flesh? This question probably did not occur to Fox at first, but by degrees he and the other Quakers found an answer. Men who had given up their spiritual privileges and surrendered to the flesh were not forsaken by their heavenly Teacher, but could not be treated as spiritual. They were trained by outward emblems and images, the elements of the world. The Jews were given a direct hint of the nature and purpose of their discipline; the Gentiles, through a thicker film of sense and with fewer helps, might still discover their invisible guide if they would. But these were preparations for a clearer day. Christ, the Living Word and Universal Light, appeared to men and showed in his own person what he was carrying on in all hearts: subduing the flesh, keeping himself separate from the world, submitting to death. This manifestation began a new dispensation. Sensible emblems were no longer to block man's view of his Lord, national distinctions were to be abolished, and men might be treated as belonging to a higher state than the one they lost in Adam, and might reach a perfection that did not exist in Adam.

The Quakers found Scriptural testimony to this on every side. Stripped of the fantastical dress it sometimes wore, it was not, few readers will think, forced or unnatural. The Prophets announced a dispensation marked above all by spirituality and universality. The Sermon on the Mount plainly annulled earlier rules and maxims and put a spiritual principle in their place. Our Lord constantly declared that he came to establish a kingdom, and that it was to be within us. The Evangelists announced that his parables uncovered mysteries hidden from the foundation of the world, and he himself said he would show his disciples more plainly of the Father. Galatians says a spiritual covenant had succeeded the formal Jewish one. Ephesians says an economy hidden for ages and generations was now made known to the Apostles by the Spirit. Philippians and Hebrews urge pressing on to perfection, evidently on the ground of the new position of those addressed. These are only samples of what every page of the New Testament seemed to the Quakers to show: that our Lord came to bring in a universal Light, to establish a perfectly spiritual Kingdom, and to encourage men to seek a perfectly spiritual Life.

III\. The very idea of this constitution implies that men are brought under a directly divine government or influence. Those who yield to the light and become members of the spiritual kingdom recognise this influence in all they do. They will not move without it, and will move anywhere at its bidding. Sacrificing all personal inclinations, energies and will, in short self-annihilation in its highest form, is their duty and privilege. So they become fit to utter the divine voice and ready to do the divine will.

For this doctrine the Quakers would plead John the Baptist's announcement of the baptism of the Holy Spirit and fire as the great promise of the new covenant. They would point to the Apostles' ignorance until they received the gift from on high, and to the silence and waiting enjoined on them until it came. They would cite the whole course of the Apostolic history, which shows the first ministers of Christ believing they acted under immediate inspiration and could not act without it. And they would add the principle, so often asserted and everywhere implied, that the kingdom was to be everlasting and that those who first saw it established were to be patterns for all who followed.

#### Section II.—Objections to the Quaker Theology considered.

I am far from saying that the early Quakers acknowledged no theological principles except these three. In a sense they admitted most of the doctrines that other men put into creeds or articles. But these three principles determined that sense. They were the ones the Quakers had truly realised in their minds. The rest hung loosely about them, and at times was heartily recognised and at times almost rejected, depending on whether it seemed to fit the primary truths or contradict them. So these three doctrines make up the positive theology of the Quakers, and their system has been deduced from them. Before I ask what that system is, and how far it is legitimately connected with these principles, I will say briefly why I cannot join some prominent opponents of Quakerism in denouncing them. I believe they are either truths or hints of truths that are vital and important.

I\. Three objections are usually brought against Fox's doctrine of the Inward Light, or the Indwelling Word. First, it is said to be mystical. Second, it is said to be unscriptural. Third, it is said to rest on no fact or authority except that of an ignorant mechanic and his credulous disciples.

1\. I will not dodge the first charge by saying that "mystical" can mean anything or nothing, or that it has been used against the best-established principles of physics as well as of morals, or that if mystical means mysterious then all science is mystical. I will give the word a sense that may be legitimate and is at least common. Mysticism is often the name for the tendency to treat the feelings, states of consciousness and temperaments of individual men as sacred, when that sacredness belongs only to truths of universal character that can be brought to a universal test. Religious men in all ages have been prone to this, and I know no records with more frequent instances of it than those of the early Quakers. But on this definition, and I know no other that clearly condemns mysticism, the question is whether the doctrine we are considering is essentially unmystical, and whether we might almost call it *the* antagonist of mysticism. It disclaims exclusive ownership more vehemently than almost any doctrine, and it submits more directly to a universal test. Fox did not say, "This light is mine." He said, "It is yours as much as mine; it is with you, and in the healthiest, truest, soberest states of your mind you know that it is." So the principle stood in marked contrast to the peculiar experiences and interpretations on which he often laid so much stress. It showed its difference from them by its effects. It also gained some sanction from being forced on the conviction of men whose characteristic weaknesses would have led them to an entirely different conclusion.

2\. The notion that the doctrine is unscriptural has drawn support partly from the opinion that Fox and his followers habitually disparaged the Scriptures, and partly from Fox's own admission that he knew the doctrine before he saw it in the Bible, though he later learned to support it from the Bible. I will consider later how far the general charge is true. It does not affect this case, because they appeal not to a few isolated texts but to the whole tenor and context of the inspired volume. Nor do I see anything in Fox's account of how he came to this principle that differs from what writers most opposed to him commonly say: that once they were spiritually awakened, the Bible, which had been a dead letter, seemed full of meaning, and the only wonder was that they had not seen it before. I think this language is simple, reasonable, true to the experience of most earnest men, and no insult to the Bible. It is not incompatible with the belief that studying the Bible may have been one of the chief means of calling forth the capacity that makes its words understandable. And nothing about the path another man followed should stop us from asking whether his view of a book sheds light on it and makes its contents more coherent. I have given a few reasons that lead others, and compel me, to believe that denying Fox's doctrine makes the scheme, spirit and letter of Scripture all perplexing. If more proof were needed, I would find it in the violent and tortuous expedients critics have used to escape, as they say, the absurdities of mystical interpretation. For example, I hear a grave, learned and (so far as hostility to Socinianism earns the name) orthodox interpreter suggest that the *Λόγος*, "the Word," in the first verse of John's Gospel means only "the one spoken of," the person promised. He supports this with the question of John's disciples, "Are you the one who is to come?", and treats the two phrases as equivalent.
