It turned to its own use the materialist notions of the age that governments originate in compacts and conventions. Finally, it forced the sages to admit that the government of reason must begin in outbreaks of popular fury, and to join the people in founding society on a declaration of individual rights.

The allies soon became enemies. The philosophers could do nothing with their theories, and the poorer men tried other weapons. The onlookers, terrified, began to ask whether the notions they had adopted as the highest discoveries of the enlightened intellect had been falsely deduced. Could law and government really have been born as the eighteenth-century teachers supposed? Must they not have had a higher source? Was it not necessary to believe that some mysterious power upheld them? These thoughts, stirring especially in the Protestant nations, prepared men to listen to Burke. He told them, as one who knew, that law rests on deep invisible principles, not on philosophical maxims; that it is to be feared and reverenced as above us, not treated as our creature and servant; and that if its existence and awful derivation are trifled with or denied, it will prove its power and take its revenge. This teaching, so unlike what the last age was used to, was received by many wondering nobles and ecclesiastics as a new truth given to defend their houses and lands. Others welcomed it more warmly and thankfully, as the application to new circumstances of a doctrine familiar to all great thinkers and delivered with peculiar power by the noblest English writers. Many now confess how much Burke, an adventurer, an Irishman, a philosopher, did to restore the tone of English feeling among men of action and of meditation, in the upper and middle classes. His influence was not confined to this country. The deep historical researches of Niebuhr and the jurisprudential wisdom of Savigny, if not called forth by his writings, took their direction largely from him. They would have found no readers able to appreciate them if our statesman and orator had not first prepared the soil.

The French Revolution has thus led many thoughtful persons, and many who are not, to the conviction that the doctrine behind the declaration of rights is essentially false. A man who chooses independence, chooses to be an individual, chooses the state of *nature*, can have no claims on his neighbour. To build a fellowship on independence is a monstrous contradiction, which shows itself the moment it is tried. And law, as the appointed corrector and judge of man, must derive from and rest on sanctions men regard as superhuman. But not a few, without directly opposing these doctrines, and perhaps agreeing with them as answers to Rousseau, draw from the same facts inferences most unlike them, almost incompatible with them. 'Whatever is said of the majesty and transcendent character of law,' they say, 'men plainly did set themselves above law during the Revolution and showed they could defy it. The popular *will* proved that all the terrors of law, affirmed and made more fearful by religion, could not bind it. When at last it gave way, it was not to law but to the *will* of one man, who showed that there was that in him which all the units of the nation together could not resist. Afterwards the political machine seemed to run back into its old ruts, and tradition and custom seemed to resume their sway. But the same truth was established again: all such influences prove ineffectual. A will, a despotical will, is wanted somewhere, and to this alone will men really bow. Whether or not individual men have a right, there is—(I borrow the favourite phrase of a writer who has shown this most clearly, and who turned the history of the Revolution from an abstraction into a living reality)—"a might," and this might will make itself felt, either in a whole nation or in some single person who compels the nation to acknowledge that he is meant to govern it.'

Put this way, one might fancy Hobbes speaking again in the nineteenth century. But whatever the resemblance in words, the feeling behind them is the opposite of the hard materialist's: it is reverence for *spiritual* force. The triumphant despot is not obeyed because men find it more convenient not to fight, or find one ruler less perplexing than many. They do him homage because he has the highest title and the most perfect ordination, because he was truly created to be their king. So this is another, and I think a more advanced and reasonable, form of reverence for [will]{.smallcaps} as superior to the forms of government and society, the reverence that led many to regard rule and subjection as hateful inventions of priests and monarchs. The writer of "Prometheus Unbound" and the "Revolt of Islam" preached man's freedom from all outward forms and restraints. Those who say subjection is a necessity of man's being, that he *longs* to be governed, are equally sure that he can submit only to the dominion of a man, never to the authority of an outward rule. Both differ from the sentimental teachers of the last age, who told men to follow their natures and give their good feelings and impulses fair play. Both hold that a man must *not* yield to inclination but must win a victory over his nature, or else he can be neither free himself nor lord over his fellows.

Meanwhile these notions, which in this form might be dismissed as idle dreams, are forcing themselves in another form on all practical politicians. Not only in quiet chambers but by fierce mobs the doctrine is proclaimed that Will is superior to Law, that it ought to be, and that it has the power to unmake and remake what pretends to hold it down. Anyone who attends to the phrases now current will, I think, find them far more tremendous than those heard at the start of the French Revolution. "We have a right" acknowledges some antecedent principle. In our day this language, when we hear it, translates at once into "We [will]{.smallcaps}." That is the ground of the right, and it aspires to be the ground of all things.

'And why has it not yet attained its aspiration, and what can hinder it?' asks the terrified statesman. When that question is raised, he finds politics has become an awful science. A man who confines himself to official rules and precedents, and admits nothing transcendent as his concern, can scarcely pursue it successfully. However he may laugh at metaphysics and scorn theology, he must discuss a subject touching the deepest principles of both: by what means the existence of law can be reconciled with the existence of the human will. The debate between the disciple of Rousseau and the disciple of Burke brought out the old controversy: is the nature of man good and to be trusted, as Rousseau affirms, or evil, as the Reformers said, to be kept down and triumphed over by every good man? After the French Revolution, politicians decided this in favour of the ancient opinion. But now another old scholastic controversy is brought to the same practical issue and the same adjudication: is *Man*, as the successors of the Reformers have affirmed, to be *identified* with the nature attached to him? Or, when he sinks under that nature's dominion, is he abandoning his proper state and subjecting himself to that over which he was meant to rule? If it is decided in favour of the first notion, that of modern Calvinism, the politician must invent what charms he can to lull that will to sleep, the will that has often been bound with fetters and chains and has torn the chains apart and broken the fetters, that no one has tamed, and that dwells continually in the mountains and the tombs, crying out and cutting itself with stones. If instead he admits that there is a will or spirit in man, safe and free only when it finds another will to govern it, and that in its vain effort at independence it becomes the slave of its own natural inclinations, then there is no contradiction in supposing two things. Law is meant to overawe those inclinations; and there is a spiritual government in which the man himself has a claim of citizenship, and in which he may find his rightful king.

#### Section III.—Political Movements, 2.

2\. This hint leads to another aspect of the French Revolution, its properly political one. It began with a declaration of individual rights, but it claimed to build a society on that declaration, and the society was to be *universal*. The proceedings were thoroughly French from first to last. French patriotism surged up against the invasion of the Allies, and a wish for French predominance can be traced in the nation's counsels from the start of the war to Napoleon's abdication. But I am speaking of the real principle that governed the minds of those who acted, not the nominal principles of parchment documents and pompous phrases. That real principle alone saved those documents and phrases from being wholly meaningless. It was the substitution of a universal polity for national polities. Every monstrous absurdity of its speakers, writers, and actors showed this feeling. All its achievements, whether as a republic or as concentrated in one man, tended the same way. Even the constitutions proposed one after another for France had no more to do with France than with Kamschatka. They were built on universal principles and meant for mankind.

These illustrations showed how valuable particular governments are when they are framed on general maxims. They roused the thoughtful men of Europe to study national history, and the inner life by which nations keep their identity for generations through every external change. These investigations drew light and strength from the national feelings that French propagandism and the tyranny of the universal empire called forth. Men could no longer take the histories of Voltaire, Hume, and Robertson as representing the mind of past generations. Children came to feel a bond with their fathers: they were heirs of the same soil, and had been left names, memorials, and institutions more lasting than its oaks to keep.

But this patriotic temper hardly outlasted the war in Germany and the Peninsula. The historical inquiries it had inspired were not neglected, and were valued more as their wisdom and learning became known. Yet they influenced the popular mind only by supplying armour against some of its most prevalent tendencies. Despite the solemn warnings of those who made these inquiries, schemes of universal society have been most rife among us. They pass over differences of race and language as insignificant, ridicule custom and reverence for ancestors, and annul the old constitutions that embody them. It may be well to notice two or three of them.

Philosophy in Europe has been changing, perceptibly only in recent years but since at least the beginning of this century. So it may seem surprising that Mr. Bentham, a philosopher of the last generation, formed in the school of Locke and Hobbes and aiming to put their maxims into practice, should have influenced so many people of our day. It would be hard to explain if his influence had lasted and overcome its opponents. But I think it is not impossible to see why it was felt for a time, in his own country and abroad. He came into notice after the great Rousseau experiment had been made and had led to the consequences I have described. No one could deny that Burke and the Constitutionalists had gained much for their argument from it. They seemed right and the defenders of popular government wrong. Still, there was a restless feeling that the trial might have been conducted differently and then have answered its authors' wishes. Besides, the countries of Europe, and soon the Spanish colonies of America, were left so stripped by revolution that they seemed to need some new constitution. Mr. Bentham met these feelings. He told men that the Rousseau scheme was false in its very conception. Before society exists we have no rights, and apart from combination we are good for nothing. The end of any combination is the greatest happiness of the greatest number of the units who enter it. The combination is held together by its members' mutual suspicion, and by contrivances that dispose the governing body not to violate the interests of those whose affairs it administers. With these few, comprehensible rules, he applied a very acute and painstaking intellect, first to constructing a scheme of government and legislation to fit his theory, and second to exposing in detail the contradictions and absurdities in the practice of different nations, especially his own. Young men ashamed of being thought sentimental, yet longing for the glorious commonwealth the sentimental school had promised, found it consoling to see a new society built on logic. Their elders could more easily grasp, even if unwilling to admit, the justice of his practical charge. In ordinary acts and talk they admitted no principle but his, yet they maintained institutions that principle could not justify. They were thus applying the doctrine of self-interest where it served their ends and repudiating it where it inconveniently interfered. These facts could not be denied. If Mr. Bentham could have made his system seem to concern only law and government, he would for some time have kept his disciples and kept gaining more. After the shock of the Revolution and the war, men inclined to occupy themselves with the workings of their own minds lost interest in politics and decried them as belonging to a merely outward region. So they could readily let almost any political system gain currency. But they soon found that a compact, organized political scheme must involve the questions they thought alone sacred. Social Utilitarianism could not be separated from individual Utilitarianism, nor could an ultimate end acknowledged in one region of thought be flatly denied in another. This became apparent both to the most real minds, impatient of artificial boundaries, and to the strictly logical. Poets found that their art could be defended only as an amusement, on the same grounds as cards or horse-racing. Religious men, however reluctant to relate such topics, were driven to ask whether the doctrine of Paley and Bentham could be reconciled with the Sermon on the Mount, and if not, which was to be given up. The school was thus unexpectedly assailed by all the new and strange thoughts on literature, metaphysics, and the spiritual universe that had been working themselves out in minds across Europe while Mr. Bentham was busy in his study with the rationale of evidence. Having nothing to answer with, it could only sound a retreat. At whatever risk of inconsistency, theoretical or practical, it tried to defend its philosophy by confining it within narrow limits. Even there it has no safe dwelling. Man's desire for a universal polity grows stronger daily and is bound ever more to deep feelings and passions, ever less to the mere calculating understanding. Yet the Benthamites appeal only to that understanding, and by it would fashion the whole scheme of human life and of the universe. Their right thing is that the will of the majority be omnipotent. But what calculus have they found for measuring the strength in that word *will*, or for knowing what becomes of all their theories and axioms of legislation once it has won supremacy?

Far more profound in conception, and I think more interesting in detail, was the system proclaimed in France about twelve years ago under the name of St. Simonianism. The Benthamites discarded all *national* distinctness, or treated it as accident. But one circumstance of man's condition could not be wholly accidental or the fruit of bad legislation: men exist in families. It seems that the most fortunate societies have recognized family life most and defined its limits most accurately. The Benthamites took no notice of this and built a society on the hypothesis that mankind is an aggregate of individual atoms. The St. Simonians saw at once that this was a practical delusion. As long as men's notions are what they are, and the morality that sustains and is sustained by them lasts, men will keep acting, speaking, and voting by families, not by heads. So the aristocratic idea intrudes, and perfect democratic fellowship is impossible. I respect the feeling, whatever its cause, that kept the English Utilitarians from looking boldly into the gulf this thought opens. I am glad they dared not sacrifice their moral impulses to logical consistency, and I can see how they persuaded themselves they *were* consistent, since it was not for the greatest happiness of the greatest number that family ties should be sacrificed. Yet I must maintain that if a universal society is to be built, on the Utilitarian maxim or on the St. Simonian maxim, "each according to his capacity," it is an indispensable first step to extinguish domestic feelings, associations, and sympathies, all the laws that uphold them, and all acknowledgment of relationship as a significant fact. The deepest mind ever lodged in a mortal, searching with the noblest and purest aims for the foundation of universal society, could find no escape from this conclusion. Every new project for actually establishing such a society has supplied fresh evidence that these must be its conditions if human hands are to build it.

But the St. Simonians felt that a universal society, even of the kind they imagined, even a universal bank, could not be established by mere human hands. This was another sign of the deeper wisdom at work amid their extravagances and contradictions. As they repeated "each according to his capacity," they must have asked: where shall we find the judge of capacities? Where dwells the seeing eye that will perceive in each the mighty power in him and assign him his rightful vocation? The question carried them into a mysterious region. This commonwealth must have some supernatural foundation and some supernatural superintendence; on any other hypothesis it was inconceivable. Once convinced of that, they felt it a duty, or what seemed the same thing to them a logical necessity, to invent supernatural machinery and put on the airs of inspired men. This fraud brought every kind of absurdity and falsehood after it, and under their weight the system quickly sank. French philosophers were not yet ready for an Apollonius in the nineteenth century. They had not yet learned to feel as Porphyry felt, that there was a kingdom in the world that could not be opposed without the help of some mythical hero. Perhaps, if the vision of such a kingdom becomes clearer and more threatening, some will be driven to that resource. St. Simonianism will then reappear under another name and form, to see whether it can satisfy the inextinguishable longing of human hearts for a human fellowship.

The socialism of Mr. Owen is wholly unlike St. Simonianism in all its more striking and philosophical features, and for that very reason it may win wider popularity for a time. A benevolent person set up a factory in a district of Scotland on a principle that made it a blessing, not a curse, to its workers and the neighbourhood. One who had shared in the experiment, though he did not originate it, adopted the plausible notion that a similar arrangement might suit all our manufacturing districts. How to deal with the population concentrated there is the most awful problem facing the modern politician, so anyone with even a suggestion, especially one drawn from experience, deserved a patient hearing. Mr. Owen showed that men under a laborious, kindly, self-denying superintendence, with certain contrivances, would be happier than those treated as animals capable of producing a certain quantity of cotton twist. The demonstration was valuable even though the result could have been anticipated. But by a process, alas, most natural, he went on to the conviction that the whole secret lay in his particular machinery. By another step he concluded that such machinery could by itself produce every desirable moral result. Once that Rubicon was crossed, it took only a mind more generalising, daring, and self-conceited than most, held back by little historical knowledge and few intellectual obstacles from experiments in disorganising society, to produce a preacher of the doctrine that men are mere creatures of circumstances, who can be wholly reformed by readjusting them. The corollaries worked themselves out gradually, without intellectual subtlety, as obstructions to the scheme appeared. Whatever principles, practices, or institutions among men are tied to the idea of a will or spirit in us that might be superior to circumstances must be given up. All forms of religious faith inevitably fall under this. But the Owenites have been permitted to enforce a discovery of the highest practical value, though they were not the first to make it: Marriage is in the same category. Its meaning or validity cannot be maintained, logically or practically, when a spiritual principle and spiritual obligations are denied.

Owenism may be described as an attempt, on a larger scale than Benthamism, to apply to all society the maxims already adopted by certain classes within it. Worship of circumstances is the habit of feeling the easy and comfortable part of mankind naturally fall into. Their inward thought is that their houses will last for ever, and that makes them averse to change and sceptical of any invisible government. Now the poor say, "We, too, will acknowledge circumstances to be all in all, we will cast away belief in the invisible, this world shall be the only home we dwell in." Such language may well appal all who hear it. To one who sympathizes with the poor it is fearful because of what it shows they are ready to abandon. To one who does not, it is fearful because of what it shows they are ready to take from him. Yet the force of these assertions lies in the very point where they are anti-socialist. It is the "*we will*" that gives them all their meaning, and it is that which gives the dry chips of Mr. Owen's theory the semblance of life. He protests against the existence of that to which he owes his own pertinacity and all the effects it has ever produced.

So combination in its simpler forms is much more truly the characteristic of our times, and much more truly fearful because it carries more elements of real power, than all the schemes for reconstructing the universe. I mean combination to help the will of the majority and enforce its right to be heard and to rule: trades' unions and chartist unions. I use language from English experience, but I fear it is quite intelligible to other nations, at least to Frenchmen. This combination is not stripped of religious sympathies, but with piteous fury strives to seize and appropriate them for its own purpose. No doubt every one of these schemes embodies some truth that cannot be lost. The greatest happiness of the greatest number may be the most idle and insignificant of formulas until each of its nouns and adjectives has been translated, yet it must contain a meaning that will somehow be realised. The phrase "each according to his capacity" points to a persuasion, which we cannot afford to lose, that gifts are suited to particular vocations and offices. The idea of co-operation, on which Owen dwells, is of wonderful depth and importance. But each scheme is chiefly remarkable as the shrine of a feeling it cannot satisfy and of a conviction it labours to stifle. The feeling is that a universal society is needful to man. The conviction is that if there is such a society, its characteristic distinction must be the treatment of man as a voluntary or spiritual being.

#### Section III.—Political Movements, 3.

3\. Among the notions Rousseau scattered about the world, some of the most startling concerned *education*. The author of "Émile" said that Plato's commonwealth is simply a scheme of education. When the true commonwealth rises out of the ruins of the old society, he said, the question of how education is to be conducted will swallow up all others. Men who started from opposite points to Rousseau, and went in different directions from him and from each other, came to hold this opinion. People began to believe that education would take the place of prisons, penitentiaries and hangmen, and that statesmen above all others were bound to care about it. The French Revolution shook these feelings. As the awfulness and dignity of Law were acknowledged again, the idea of replacing its punishments with gentler influences was scorned. The educational doctrine was treated as part of the sentimental creed about the goodness of human nature. That creed had been proved absurd, since men had been seen to do and wish to do very little without prisons or guillotines, so its inferences had to be abandoned too. These feelings seldom took shape as definite thoughts or arguments, but they were powerful. To them, I think, we may attribute the dislike of popular education shown by supporters of existing institutions, especially in England.

But both sides in the controversy had forgotten one point. Education must *henceforth*, said the disciple of the new school, be the great agent in the world. Nonsense, replied the English country gentleman; we will keep to the old ways and do as our forefathers did. But what were the old ways? History shows that our forefathers valued education as much as Rousseau did. They believed prisons and penitentiaries were useless without it, and that it had powers never entrusted to them. They had no thought of confining it to any class, since they were always providing for the training of youths whose chief qualification was poverty. This discovery was fatal to the argument against the philosophers, and for a time it settled the practical question in their favour. England's old educational institutions had been abused through carelessness or selfishness, and on the continent they had been destroyed by revolutionary violence. In both, multitudes were growing up with the new notions of popular power and will, and a new unwillingness to bow to authorities merely because they were established. Jails could not be provided for all, or if they could, it might in time be a question who would be the jailers. So statesmen began to say that we must have fresh schools. The old ones have served well enough to train those with a national position, but they were not meant for the masses who have none. They may train those who want instruction and will make sacrifices for it, but they are no use to those who hardly think of it as a blessing or know what it means.

These thoughts have, I think, greatly changed the minds of all men on the subject. I say *all*, because the change is as remarkable in those who called education a panacea as in those only lately awakened to its necessity. The former used to urge how much the poor would gain from having intellectual cultivation within reach: new pleasures to command, temptations to avoid. The latter rebutted this with plausible appeals to fact. Did the boys in the schools acquire these tastes? Were they the better for their knowledge? Was the population more refined or more moral? I do not know where such controversies could end, or how much statistics may have been twisted on each side to prove the theorem. But the advocates of education now say: look at these masses of human beings. The question is not what you should do to amuse or benefit them, but what you *must* do to govern them and keep them from destroying the land and themselves. The ordinary resources of government have failed. Religion has proved inadequate when it serves only as a support to law. You must try some other means. You may laugh at the idea that a silent moral influence like education could be enough, but history and these living masses laugh at the idea that your physical appliances are enough. This is quite different language, and it is not easy to answer. The only answer is the sleepy resolve not to think about it until a revolution forces us to.

This change of language involves other changes that may not be obvious. There may be a hundred differences over what should be taught and who should teach it. But those who defend education on these grounds agree that, whatever the right instruments, the aim is to gain dominion over the *will* of these masses. The phrasing matters little. Earnest and able defenders of education may be loath to adopt the conviction I noted that many political students had reached by comparing the theories and events of the last fifty years. That conviction speaks of man as a mysterious being whose natural inclinations, if followed, make him a lawbreaker and an enemy of his fellows, but who may be raised above them to a true freedom, above the penalty of laws because he has no wish to break them. Such expressions may seem strange and fantastical to them, and unlike the school they were trained in. Yet they may mean nearly what those who use such odd mystical talk mean. By many words and acts they confess that they regard education as a wondrous power acting in a wondrous way. It is not a charm, and it is not inconsistent with man's constitution. It is a power that is somehow to reach a faculty or principle, call it what you like, that swords and clubs cannot reach. There is, I think, very general agreement on this, however diverse the elements of the agreement. Take those who say it is hopeless to give religious instruction to all the members of a nation, so that (what they call) secular instruction must come first. They defend this on two grounds. One is that the perils of leaving the people untaught are infinite, with threats of popular outrage everywhere that the legislature cannot put down. The other is that there must be something common to all men, something all can receive, and this cannot be sect-notions and opinions, which clearly do not belong to all. Taken simply as it stands, this argument leads to reflections quite unlike any the ablest treatises on education of the last century would naturally suggest. It sets us thinking what kind of power can address a whole body of human creatures and call out in each of them what gives him the rights of *Man* and makes him a fellow-worker with his brethren. Sad experience has convinced thoughtful people that the secret does not lie in the mechanical contrivances for gathering children in a school, which were offered a few years ago as the perfect cure for the nation's evils. These organize and discipline masses into a stupid material consistency, or now and then rouse the powers of one or two clever pupils to a dangerous self-exalting activity. They do not give the whole body a quiet life circulating through each member. The Protestant systems and sects, as I have said, are also found inadequate. They scarcely recognize that in the pupil which can be spoken to and called forth, and they divide instead of harmonising. Still there seems to be such a power somewhere. Who will tell us where?

4\. The method used to cut this knot explains the last remarkable peculiarity of our modern political views. All the bodies that profess a voluntary character have proved unequal to conducting education. So it has seemed inevitable that each nation's government should undertake to form and superintend it. Anyone who has soberly considered how to meet this dire necessity will not be surprised that hundreds see it as the only refuge from despair. We may have to give up many cherished notions of personal and domestic liberty. It may be painful that we in the nineteenth century have no better resource than one we learnt to think unsatisfactory and dangerous in the old Greek republics. Still a wise lover of his species will not be stopped from adopting what he is sure is the only practical relief. But some perplexing reflections intrude before he reaches that certainty. Is it not strange that we assert this marvellous power for the state just when there is the most reluctance to acknowledge even the powers that plainly belong to it? Is not the Will of the multitude asserting its independence of the civil power, and is this not the very exigency we are devising a remedy for? Have not most European governments been hastily stripping off the spiritual properties and functions they claimed in earlier times? Are not those called illiberal that hesitate to do so? Is it not inconsistent that these governments should at the same moment assume what is, by their own confession, an authority over the spirits of men in the strictest sense? I say *by their own confession*, because we do not escape the difficulty by saying the state shall have charge only of *secular* education, leaving the rest to more peculiar and individual interests. Is not our aim in education to gain an influence over human hearts that are cherishing fearful dreams of independence and defiance? Is not this the only plea for the state's interference, that its existence is endangered by ignorance and self-will? And must not the education the state superintends aim above all, by whatever means seem likeliest, and whatever epithet you give them, at accomplishing this?

I fancy the practical statesman will be continually haunted by this contradiction, and perplexed far more in action than in meditation. People cry out for a power that is not merely legal or punitive but acts directly on the human spirit, so that legal and punitive sanctions may appeal to the consciences of men rather than the fears of brutes. To satisfy them, you inspire the state with this power, or at least force it into acts implying it. Yet the state is saying all the time that it can deal with nothing internal and can take notice only of overt acts. People also cry out for a universal body in which men are regarded as human beings and not merely as members of a local society. To satisfy them, you insist that the government of a local society take on a universal human character. All the new schemes for managing the world bear witness that it never has had and never can have such a character, and that its only security lies in its distinctions and limitations. Still the difficulty remains. If there is no spiritual universal society, and every attempt to create one in this nineteenth century has been abortive, the state must take on the appearance and do the duties of one. It must do so at any hazard of inconsistency, despite every danger to individual liberty, and despite the strong and growing feeling that it is not competent.

## PART II. *OF THE CATHOLIC CHURCH AND THE ROMISH SYSTEM*.

### CHAPTER I. RECAPITULATION.

[Our]{.smallcaps} conclusion seems to be this. The principles asserted by the religious societies formed in Europe since the Reformation are solid and imperishable. The systems that embodied them were faulty from the start, have fulfilled their purpose less and less as they aged, and now show clear signs of approaching dissolution. When I spoke of modern philosophical movements, I mentioned popular statements that at first look much like this conclusion. One is the doctrine that systems, religions and churches are dying, but that they were the clothing of important ideas which will outlive them, and which wise men should note, preserve and perhaps re-clothe. I cannot hold that doctrine; if this book means anything, I must oppose it. Still, a conscientious reader may find it hard to see where it and mine part ways. To make the link between what I have said and what I am about to say intelligible, I must relieve him of that difficulty. The best way is to remind him of the evidence we have met along the way.

I\. On the authority of the old Quaker books, and against one of their modern teachers, I held the fundamental doctrine of Quakerism to be this. Man is a twofold creature. He has inclinations toward sensible things, but by trusting in the divine Word, to whom he is united, he can rise above them to spiritual life and communion. The people most unlike the early Quakers are modern philosophers, artists and politicians. Yet we found people of these classes, by various routes, adopting the very maxim that was the great obstacle to believing Fox's principle. Religious people said it cannot be true, because man as such, apart from a special religious calling and impulse, is not spiritual. The philosophers of the last generation said it cannot be true, because man is not capable of such a calling and impulse at all, being simply flesh and blood. Those who speak for the enlightenment of our time would disown both opinions. They say man as man has spiritual powers and is a spiritual creature.

Many who use that language would present it as a doctrine stripped of its old form and reduced to its pure essence. "In acknowledging a spiritual life in man," they would say, "we keep Fox's meaning and only remove the phraseology that earlier religious tradition put on it." I am not arguing whether that is so. I am showing that our observations led to the opposite conclusion. A man who believes he has spiritual capacities is just the man who must ask under what conditions they exist. That was Fox's problem. The sense of upward tendencies in himself, constantly restrained and resisted, gave him not peace but torment. We may talk in general about our spiritual power. But the moment we realize it practically, amid the contradictions it lives under in this world, it gets tangled with awful responsibility, with the vision of an unknown world, with the certainty of moral evil, and we are glad to escape into materialism. Since that escape is now closed, we must ask where these desires and impulses come from, what upholds them, and where they tend. Those questions lead straight to the principle Fox asserted. A system may omit it or substitute another, but every man really engaged in the world's conflict must face it. Modern thinkers constantly show they lack it, especially when they speak of self-consciousness and its necessity, misery and falsehood. The words "Not I but Christ in me" answer these perplexities, and we may search heaven and earth before finding another answer. If I am asked why, then, I say the Quaker system has proved inadequate, my answer is this. It has failed to give Fox's idea a clear, definite expression. It has reduced it nearly to a vague synonym for the notion that we have spiritual capacities or feelings, and it has *not* shown men the object on whom Fox said those capacities and feelings were to be exercised.

The ordinary philosopher knows little of Fox. He is more anxious to show that he has kept all that is precious in the great Reformer's teaching. Our mere men of letters revere Leo, think Erasmus protested as much as the abominations of his time required, and regard Luther, who knew nothing of statues and wrote poor Latin, with positive aversion. The more earnest men, especially those who believe European society has been progressing from darkness to light, speak of Luther as one who worked mightily to overthrow opinions resting on mere authority and tradition, and who prepared the way for thoughts he himself would have rejected with horror. I am not speaking of the ignorant declaimers who praise Luther for exalting the understanding over faith. I mean those who actually know his writings, and who know he deserves that praise about as much as Brutus deserved canonization by the French sansculotte for slaying the aristocrat Julius Cæsar. They would think it an ill compliment to say a man lacked faith and wished to destroy it in others. They readily admit that "A man is justified by faith" is more characteristic of Luther than his opposition to popes or masses, and that the acts of his outward life all referred more or less directly to that principle. But, they say, taken out of the swaddling bands of the sixteenth century and allowed to move freely, the principle means this: a man's inward state, not his performance of prescribed acts or even his worthy outward conduct, entitles him to be called good. Not what he seems, nor even what he does, but what he *is*, makes him a right and true man. The Church of that day had set this truth at nought, and by the vehemence with which Luther declared it and made men listen, he earned his chief claim to mankind's gratitude.

I am well convinced that the Romanists had practically denied this principle, and that Luther was the most powerful instrument in reasserting it. But we saw reason to believe that George Fox held the doctrine of inward righteousness as strongly as the German, and yet that there was a marked difference between them. Fox urged his disciples to exercise faith in a spiritual Being, the Lord of their spirits. Luther delighted to declare that this Being had actually taken human flesh and died a human death, and had by these acts redeemed us from a curse and justified us before his Father. Luther's admirers constantly say he was straightforward and practical, impatient of abstractions, and fond of plain homely images. Here, it seemed to us, lay the secret of those qualities. He was not speaking of a fantastic Being or a mere idea, nor even *merely* of an object for spiritual apprehension, though he was that in the highest degree. He spoke of one who had identified himself with men and, by the series of outward acts the creed announces, had established his human as well as his divine character. It struck us that this, the strictly Lutheran characteristic, is needed to complete the Quaker doctrine, which without it soon turns to vapour. Whether or not that is so, this peculiarity of Luther's preaching enabled him to overthrow existing superstitions and so, as we are told, to precede greater deliverances. The Romanists certainly recognised the fact of the Incarnation. But their notions of inward and inherent righteousness, and the practices built on them, had stripped it of significance. It had ceased to witness to any man of what he was. By affirming the reality and significance of this fact, Luther got rid of the impositions on understanding and conscience that practical unbelief in it had made possible. So it seemed to us that we do not merely strip Luther of his dress, but destroy the man, when we make him witness for a principle and not for a fact. We do not keep the quality that made him a reformer and discard what he shared with those he reformed. We take away the very source of his reforming power, and leave ourselves, if all history is not a delusion, in danger of falling back under the sensible tyrannies he freed us from. If I am asked again what my objection to the Lutheran system is, I answer: only this. It does not bear witness to the all-importance of the fact Luther said was all-important. It teaches us to believe in justification by faith instead of in a Justifier, and substitutes for Christ a notion or scheme of Christianity.

Quakerism and pure Protestantism belong chiefly, if not wholly, to individual life and experience. Unitarianism, we found, was altogether different: it took men from self-reflection to thoughts about nature and God. Yet it has changed its complexion as men's views of themselves have changed. It applied material standards to the Infinite while the habit of the time was to regard men as purely material. When that habit ceased, it began to decry ordinary theological language as too earthly and definite. Here, perhaps, is a system that exactly answers the philosophers' demand, readily dropping one period's dress for another's. But will it be said that *this* is merely a change of dress? Those who just now called the acknowledgment of man's spiritual powers the very *essence* of religion cannot so change their note as to call it an accident. By their own showing, Unitarianism has not kept its identity, and changing popular opinion has abolished its very substance. But it seemed to us that this was not so, and that it has a principle which is constantly and invariably true. Its hold on earnest minds came from declaring the unity of God, the absolute love of God, and the existence of a good and pure state for mankind to be primary truths that no experience or dogma can alter or set aside. These are eternal principles, not subject to changes of costume or fashion, and needed by man at all times. Our objection to the Unitarian system was that it represented these truths feebly and miserably, and even contradicted them in practice as no other system ever has. That charge applies to the modern scheme even more strongly than to the old. The whole virtue of Unitarianism lay in asserting God's existence as distinct from men's thoughts and apprehensions, whereas the later teachers keep drifting toward confusing our own spiritual "nature" with the Being it acknowledges. From there the step to worshipping the separate feelings and moods of that "nature," and then to prostration before them as idols, is very short. A strange apotheosis for Unitarianism!

I contend, then, that the principles of Fox, Luther and the Unitarians are too strong and vital to bear the imprisonment the various systems have put them in. But I do not think they will become more true and vital when they lose their religious and personal character and are translated into terms of a philosophical theory. I believe they will then lose all their preciousness, and reach the perfect impotence and insignificance to which so far they have been only partly reduced.

II\. Even in their strength, though, these principles have shown a disposition to clothe themselves in some form. Only in times of great weakness do they rest content as notions or opinions in individual minds. So we must ask what this tendency means, and how it may be satisfied when the systems that arose from it no longer satisfy it. To answer, we must turn to another class of facts. The second distinguishing Quaker tenet was that a spiritual and universal kingdom is established in the world. We can conceive, though with great difficulty, that the doctrine of the Indwelling Word might have been received merely as a doctrine, the governing law of individual lives. But this tenet ceases to be a principle for individuals if it is only for them. That there should be such a kingdom, and an honest man who believes it not ask what the conditions of citizenship in it are, is incredible. We traced the origin of the Quaker society and system to this conviction. By joining the society, Fox's disciples sought a place in the kingdom. By adopting the system, they interpreted its nature and laws to themselves and others. So our main question about the society was whether it answers to this character, or even still claims to. About the system we asked what explains the failure to which the body's history so clearly witnesses. I will not dwell on the answer, beyond noting that a resolute avoidance of forms was a main characteristic of the Quaker system, and a tendency to formalism one of the most striking characteristics of the Quaker body. My present aim is to remark on the faith that made Quakerism necessary, not on its good or evil features.

Was this faith new, and was Fox the first to proclaim it? We found a very strong and distinct acknowledgment of a spiritual kingdom among the Reformers as well as the Quakers. It worked so powerfully in Luther's mind, and was so tied to his recognition of a divine Person, a divine Man, as the object of all trust and allegiance, that he was most reluctant to introduce any theory or scheme of doctrine that might become a substitute for it. Yet we found that it finally drove him and the other Reformers to set up little Churches or kingdoms of their own, since they could not imagine how else God's purpose could be accomplished. And Fox's protest in the next age was provoked partly by the unspiritual character of these bodies and their evident inadequacy to express Christ's spiritual kingdom. It was provoked partly also by the importance the Reformers and their followers gave to national societies, and the confusion that seemed to have arisen between them and the universal body.

This idea of a spiritual and universal kingdom was not new in the seventeenth century, but may it not have become obsolete in the nineteenth? The history of Unitarianism was an important link in the evidence. In the last century the idea was distinctly and formally repudiated by those most admired for wisdom and enlightenment, and man was excluded from any concern in such a kingdom, having no faculties to take cognizance of it. At the same time the idea of a very comprehensive *world*, including all nations, systems and religions, became prevalent and was set against the sects of Christendom. So one half of the belief of earlier ages, the half Protestants had regarded least, was trying to assert itself under new conditions, to the exclusion of the other half. If the Roman empire from Augustus to Diocletian was not a sufficient type of this all-tolerant, all-including *world*, the French Empire, which inherited and carried out the speculations of the last generation, was a fair specimen of what it must be. While that Empire spread philanthropy through Europe, we saw the other element of the idea of a kingdom for mankind, which philanthropy had cast aside as unnecessary, slowly reappear in several quarters. First came a religious awakening: men became strongly convinced that a spiritual power is at work among them. This brought a greater value for personal religion. Then it brought a desire among those who felt it for combination and fellowship in spiritual objects. Finally it raised the question whether such a combination must not have a spiritual foundation and be connected with belief and worship. Next, various philosophical men showed a new habit of thinking about the constitution of our race. They tended to see man as essentially spiritual, and so to conclude that his highest acts must be spiritual. With this faith grew another, that there must be a region for those acts, a world adapted and formed for them, since they cannot merely turn in upon themselves. We could find no clear account of this world except that it was the universe around us, which our eyes and ears take in. And though that universe was proclaimed the great inheritance of mankind, we could not learn that more than a few gifted poets and sages had any right of admission to its meanings and mysteries. The philosophers had another difficulty: does a distinct spiritual world exist at all, or is it only created out of this common world by a class endowed with faculties for the purpose? Another set of deep and earnest thinkers decided this peremptorily. They seemed to have proved that there is something man did not create, to which he must in some way refer all his acts and thoughts, and which must be assumed as their ground.

Meanwhile, the most eager and passionate demands for a universal constitution that men as men could enter came not from religious or philosophical men but from labourers, handicraftsmen and serfs. The nature of this constitution had been discussed again and again, and its settlement had not been left to discussion but put to the most severe practical tests. Three inferences seemed to follow. First, every modern attempt to construct a universal society had been defeated by men's determination to assert their own *wills*. Second, the true universal society must neither overlook those wills nor treat them as an inconvenient accident, a friction to be regretted and allowed for, but must assume them as the very principle and explanation of its existence. Third, men cannot be content with a spiritual society that is not universal, or with a universal society that is not spiritual. This doctrine, then, cannot be called obsolete or turned into a mere philosophical notion. Time has strengthened it, and there is more need now than ever that it be satisfied in practice, not in theory.

III\. These observations suggest that the spiritual and universal society must be involved in the very idea of our human constitution; or rather, that it must be that constitution, by which we realize there is a humanity and that we form a kind. But, it will be said, may we not suppose that this constitution has been making itself known gradually as civilization advances? When it is more widely diffused, each man will feel his place in it, rightly and harmoniously exercise the spiritual powers that fit him for it, and let his neighbour exercise his without molestation, even helping him. Strifes of opinion that have hindered cooperation will gradually cease, and peace and good will become general. One means to this end may be giving up the notions of the early ages, kept alive by the religious sects and systems since: that the character of this constitution has been revealed in an inspired Book, and that it is ruled in some incredible manner by a divine Person. This is the last hint I shall consider, and it leads to another class of facts we have reviewed.

The Quakers, we found, disparaged what they called the *outward letter*. They feared reverence for the Bible would interfere with belief in a Spiritual Invisible Teacher. Yet it was in the Bible that Fox learnt clearly that there is such a Teacher, and from the Bible that he preached it to others. From the Bible Luther, torn by inward conflicts, learnt not merely justification by faith but, still more, its personal form and its conception as a vital truth rather than a scholastic dogma. Calvin received the belief of Election, in its highest and purest form, from the same source. The Unitarian prized the Bible as the great witness for the Divine Unity, for God's absolute and universal love, and for the fact that mankind is under a better condition than a curse. So whenever a man has held one of these precious convictions, he has revered the Scriptures as its teacher and authority, and whenever he has carried it home to his brethren, the Scriptures have been his instrument. This is the more remarkable beside another fact the study of the systems revealed: as any of them has consolidated, the Bible has been deposed from its real dignity, even if nominally held up to admiration or worship. The Quaker turns it into a system of conceits and allegories under pretence of honoring the Spirit. He has treated it no worse than the Lutheran or Calvinist who cuts it into texts to confirm dogmas or serve as mottos for sermons, or the Unitarian who would reduce it to a collection of moral maxims. So, rather than doubting the value or authority of the book all these appeal to, because the systems are unstable and helpless, may we not say boldly this? The book revealed to each founder of a sect the aspect of the spiritual economy he was called to present. And it has been a perpetual and most embarrassing witness against the effort to compress that economy into the rules and formulas he and his followers devised to state their opinions.

But this is not enough. It is alleged that whatever may be true of religious bodies, the greatest light on our spiritual constitution has lately come from people who did not draw their knowledge from the Scriptures, and who even doubted their value and authenticity.

I have not affected to disparage the labours of philosophers, now or in the past. I have the highest respect for those who have brought to light precious truths about faculties in us that were supposed not to exist. But I have also suggested an opinion, which I am anxious to have sifted and, if false, exposed: that this is precisely the limit of their work. They have proved that we have faculties that take cognizance of spiritual transcendental objects, but not what those objects are. They have shown that we must have a spiritual constitution, but not what it is. I do not deny that we have learnt what our forefathers did not know, or knew far less well. I do not deny that experiments, failures and contradictions have made us better acquainted with what we are and want. For mankind's sake I am thankful these men were permitted to make their discoveries, apparently without seeing the truths that I think answer to them, and which show that Tantalus is not the one type of humanity. But nothing I see or hear of these writers leads me to think they have found any substitute for a *Revelation* of what man's highest necessities require. I am convinced that their teaching, honestly received, will make his cries for one more passionate. I am convinced it will be seen at last that the book which has always met its readers' cravings, and given them the glimpse of the mysterious world they needed, contains the full declaration of the state God has established for us, which we have toiled all our lives to find.

The second part of the question is bound up with the first and is perhaps the most important for our purpose. Is not the idea of a spiritual King, an actual Person superintending and ordaining the movements of the universal and spiritual society, the dream of a past age? Would not a sensible man who was also honest, and used words in their simple straightforward sense, be reluctant rather than anxious to bring it forward? Is it not obvious that every step of thought and discovery has carried us further from such a notion and left it as the proper possession of old wives and children? I have perhaps already treated this point implicitly. But I am so anxious to consider it directly that I will, at the risk of repetition and tedium, return to my former method of proof.

A belief in a direct spiritual government over the life, thoughts, acts and words of those who submit to it was, we have seen, the third distinguishing peculiarity of Quakerism. It produced so many more outward results than the other two that modern Friends think it has absorbed them. The society's system seems expressly devised to express this belief. Did it seem to us that the system was decaying because it had borne too decisive and consistent a witness to this bygone notion, or had kept it from the changes that increasing light and civilisation ought to bring? On the contrary, Quakerism's essential feebleness lay in showing the doctrine of spiritual superintendence in an inadequate, inconsistent, shrivelled form. It testified that sudden thoughts, acts and speeches, often obviously trifling, came from divine teaching and inspiration. It virtually excluded from the spiritual sphere what even Quakers must acknowledge as the most significant part of our life, the calm, orderly, continuous transactions. The Quakers thought education most important, yet on their system's terms education could not be a spiritual work.

Turn from Quakerism to its opposite, Calvinism, and we were forced to the same inference in another form. Belief in the will of God as the only spring of Good, Order and Happiness was the earnest, life-giving principle in Calvin and his disciples, and all their brave acts and good thoughts sprang from it. Had they pushed it too far? Had their system riveted the notion of a ruling Will in their minds and perpetuated it into an age when it ought naturally to have been abandoned? We were led to exactly the opposite opinion. By setting aside the idea of a human will, their system left the doctrine of a Divine will barren and meaningless. The idea of a personal Ruler disappeared, and those most anxious to assert the government of the living God became the great propagators of an atheistical Necessity.

But it may be said that these Quaker and Calvinist opinions, about the Spirit working in man and the absolute Will of God, may involve or be involved in the idea of an actual King of men, yet are not identical with it. That idea turns on the doctrine of an Incarnation: it asserts that one who is Son of Man as well as Son of God is Lord of the world and, in a higher sense, of the spiritual Society. This doctrine, it is said, is so connected with the oldest fables of the world that it must give way before the light of truth. The Unitarian of the last century thought so, and the question we discussed was how this opinion, the root of their system, affected the principles that really formed the faith of their better men.

It seemed to us that the unity of God was sacrificed, because the person acknowledged as the great object and centre of human admiration was denied to be one with the Father. The love of God was sacrificed, because it was denied that he had personally interfered on behalf of his creatures. Our being children of God was sacrificed, because nothing gave the name *Father* reality or showed it to be more than a loose and almost blasphemous figure of speech.

Accordingly, in the new Unitarianism Jesus of Nazareth is coming to be recognised as merely one of the world's heroes, perhaps the most conspicuous, perhaps not. But this belief, the last and highest discovery of the nineteenth century, takes us back to a stage of history when universal fellowship was impossible. There was a Grecian Hercules and an Egyptian Hercules; he who repealed bad laws was the hero of a country, and he who drained a marsh of a neighbourhood. Men were crying and sighing for someone to be head and prince of all these, the Lord of their race, who would rescue it from the evils it suffers as a race and connect it with the absolute Being their consciences witnessed. Is not this a strange and melancholy relapse under the name of progression!

We have, then, reasonable excuse for asking whether there is on this earth a spiritual and universal kingdom that the religious systems have not been able to supersede or destroy, that is likely to show itself when they have all perished, and with which we of the nineteenth century may have fellowship. As a preface to this inquiry, it seems fitting to ask whether there are traces of a spiritual constitution in the early ages of the world, and whether the books of Scripture help us to interpret them.

### CHAPTER II. INDICATIONS OF A SPIRITUAL CONSTITUTION.

[When]{.smallcaps} I spoke of the Quaker system, I noted one practical inconsistency at its root that affects all its workings. A member of the Society of Friends ought to be the conscious disciple of a Divine Teacher. Yet every child born to a Quaker is treated as a Friend until some act of rebellion costs him the title. We found much the same anomaly in the Protestant systems. Consciously justified men ought to make up the Evangelical Church, and persons conscious of a divine Election the Reformed. Yet neither has had the courage to shut their children out of *all* religious fellowship and treat them as heathens. The Anabaptists came nearest to doing so, but even in them there are clear signs of timidity and inconsistency.

When we looked at the schemes of the world drawn up by philosophers, we saw that they ran into a knot much like the one that perplexed the founders of religious sects, and that they cut it, with more or less ceremony. A universal society was nearly impossible to organise while the distinction of families remained, because such a society needs men to be so many separate units. But family life is a glaring fact that proves they are not units. They are bound together by a law that may be defied and made almost wholly ineffective, but cannot be entirely repealed.

I\. This fact that men live in families, which so troubles the inventors of systems, is perhaps the very one most likely to suggest to a plain person that mankind has a moral or spiritual constitution. We must speak of every man as being in two conditions. He is in a world of objects that present themselves to his senses. And he is a son, perhaps a brother. These two states are equally inevitable and perfectly distinct, and no artifice brings them under one law or name. The first is described by what we see, hear, handle or smell. The second is described by what we are: "I *am* a son," "I *am* a brother." So "*circumstances*" cannot be used of the one state as strictly as of the other. Everything I deal with I rightly call my circumstances, because *they stand round me*. But what is needed in an account of myself deserves another name, and we commonly call it a *relationship*. The difference grows plainer. We speak of a man *having* a bad digestion or bad hearing, and of his *being* a bad brother or bad son. Both phrases imply a want of harmony between the man and his condition. But the second says that the want of harmony need not exist: he is *voluntarily* acting as if he were not in a relation in which he nevertheless is, and must remain. We describe this inconsistency as moral evil, or by whatever equivalent phrase we have invented, for some equivalent we must have.

It might seem to follow that the family state is the *natural* one for man, and so we call the affections that go with it natural affections. But we also call the savage condition, in which man strives to be independent, the natural state of society. It is doubtful whether that deserves to be called a *state of society*, since it contradicts all states and all society. Still there is considerable justification for calling it natural. We cannot belong to a family without recognising in others, and feeling in ourselves, inclinations that tend to dissolve its bonds and set up a separate, independent life, which on a large scale we call savage or wild. Discipline keeps these inclinations down, and family affections are called out against them. So it is surely no mistake to give them the name we give to plants that spring up in uncultivated soil, unasked.

Here we have some indications of a spiritual constitution: the marks of a state designed for a voluntary creature, which *is* his whether he approves or not, and which he has a natural inclination to rebel against. But most people would mean more by the word, and ask how anything can be called spiritual that has no reference to religion. The histories and mythologies of all the peoples we know bear clear witness that men have connected the ideas of fathers, children, husbands, brothers and sisters with the beings they worshipped. That is our first, roughest observation. Searching further, we see that it is not merely *a* fact about mythology but *the* fact, without which all others are unintelligible. You may say the gods and goddesses are given all kinds of offices: they rule this town and that river, dispense this blessing or send that curse. Granted; but who are they who exercise these powers? Mythology tells of relations among them, and between them and those they favour or oppose. In later ages, studying the differences between nations' mythologies, we naturally notice soil, climate, the beauty or dreariness of the country, the rains and floods, as shaping the views the people held of their unseen rulers. It is then an easy step to think these things account for their faith, and that the histories of the gods are accidental poetic embellishments. But consider these points. The worshippers evidently felt what we call accidental to be essential. Merging the gods into natural objects was an artifice of later philosophy. Soil and climate did produce important *differences* among the objects reverenced in various nations, but their being parents, brothers and sisters was, so far as we know, *common* to all, missing only among the utterly savage, who disregard human relations. Those who explain mythology from natural phenomena must beg what they call "a law of nature," saying we are naturally inclined to ask the origin of great and remarkable objects. Yet it contradicts all experience to attribute that disposition to men whose feelings and faculties have not first been awakened somehow. A savage is little struck by any but the most glaring and alarming phenomena, and thinks even less about them. Considering all this, we may adopt the view that the simplest solution is the best: it is not our surroundings of sensible objects that lead us to think of objects we do not sensibly meet, but these perceptions come to us through our family relationships. We become more purely idolaters as those relationships are lost sight of and only the other facts of our condition regarded. A world without family relationships would have no worship; and without worship the feelings and affections of family life would have perished utterly.

II\. But is there no meaning in that savage wish for independence? Is it merely the dissolution of the family bonds meant for men, or a sign that men were meant for other bonds too, not necessarily incompatible with these? History seems to decide for the latter. As there is a worse state of society than the patriarchal, there is a better and more advanced one; man's faculties have never been properly developed except in a *national* community. Examining such a community from the Pagan world, we find its member had a more distinct feeling of himself, of his own personality, than a mere dweller in a family. This seems puzzling. In Sparta or Rome, or any commonwealth but Athens, society seems to impose the severest restraints on each man's taste, judgment and will. Yet what gives interest to the better times of these republics is the energetic purpose of their leaders, and our assurance that the same kind of purpose, in lesser degree, existed in every rank of their armies. Likewise what makes their declining ages mournful is the sense that the armies had become mere soldiers, not men. So we have evidence, from where it might least be expected, that personal feeling is bound up with the sense of national union.

Of all men the savage[^fn012] has *least* feeling of dignity and self-respect. He is emphatically a mere workman or tool, the habitual slave of his own chance necessities and inclinations, and therefore usually of other men's. Whoever understands the words "I am a brother" has taken a mighty step beyond him, even in the very respect the savage prides himself on; he is more of a person, more of a freeman. But he is not yet enough of either. To be more, he must be able to say "I am a citizen." That is the true onward step; whoever seeks freedom by any other way relapses into an independence that is only another name for slavery. Several facts, so obvious that careless students of history miss them, are closely connected with this. One is that every organised nation at its beginning has a high respect for family relations, which necessarily embody themselves in its constitution. Another is that there is a struggle between these relations and the national polity, though they form so great a part of it, because the legislator feels that each brother, husband and father is a citizen and as such comes directly under his cognizance.

[^fn012]: Of course no *ideal* savage actually exists, no one perfectly independent. The North American Indian has so many tribe feelings that admirers of savage life, taking him as the example, have argued that it is the very soil for cultivating domestic affections. Leaving aside the violent exaggerations and distortions of fact this hypothesis needs, these feelings and affections are plainly so many departures from savage perfection, so many threats of degeneracy into the social and civilized condition. The true savage is Caliban; the nearest approach to him is probably in New Holland.

In Sparta the principle of family life, though clearly recognised, was largely sacrificed to the Laws. In Athens the legislator, anxious to leave men to themselves, allowed an independence to grow that proved incompatible with both family relations and national society. In Rome the legislation was so finely interwoven with the family principle that when that grew weak, the commonwealth inevitably fell.

These facts lead us to ask what this legislation means, where its power lies, and how it is both connected with and different from these relationships. First, Law takes each man apart from his fellows. It addresses him with a *Thou*, makes him feel an eye fixed on his doings and a penalty hanging over him. In this view it is the direct opposite of a *relationship*, which binds us to each other and makes us feel we cannot exist apart. But second, Law denounces the acts that make union and fellowship impossible, the acts that come from men's determination to live as if independent of each other, making self-pleasing their end. It declares to each man that he is in a fellowship and must do nothing inconsistent with it. So the desire for individuality, the great foe of family relationship, is the very thing Law threatens and curses even while treating men as distinct persons. A nation, then, like a family, seems to have some characteristics of a spiritual constitution. If "spiritual" is taken as modern philosophers use it, we have abundant proof that where there is no feeling of national union, the exercise of *intellectual* power is precarious and imperfect. If it is taken as *voluntary*, we find a constitution evidently meant for creatures with wills: men do not create it for themselves, it may be violated, every man is naturally inclined to violate it, and "bad citizen" expresses moral reprobation as "bad father or mother" does. And are there *religious* feelings connected with national life, as with family life? The old mythology answers just as decisively. If the Homeric gods were fathers, brothers and husbands, they were also kings; the one character is as prominent and essential as the other. The former may have been the more ancient, which would explain scholars' notion that traces of an earlier worship appear in the Iliad. By Homer's time the domestic and kingly characters were joined, and the gods' *offices* connected with *nature*, though gradually mingling with these characters and threatening to become identical with them, remained distinct from them. The princes of Agamemnon's league felt there must be higher princes than themselves; they could use no authority and take no counsel except in that belief. And though they spoke of these rulers as compelling clouds and winds, they did not regard that as higher or more real than putting wisdom and spirit into Diomed and arming Hector for the fight. So the leaders were always types of the gods in every country that had reached a national polity. Wherever such polities existed, invisible rulers were recognised, a class of men interpreted their judgments, they were invoked as guides in battle, and sacrifices were offered to avert their displeasure or win their protection.

But a time came when men's minds were awakened to something more comprehensive than either the family or the national constitution. The family belonged to all men, yet it was narrow in separating them from each other. The nation was plainly exclusive: limited to a small locality, it treated everything beyond it that it could not subdue as alien, if not hostile. If this exclusion were to continue, some nation *ought* to reign, with a right to make its polity universal. Great Asiatic monarchies had swallowed up tribes and kingdoms, but they had set up a rule of mere physical force.

Might not Greece, the land of intellectual force, show that it was meant to rule all? The young hero of Macedon went out in that hope and in a few years accomplished his dream. A few years later his empire was broken in pieces; Greece was not to be lord of the world. Still, in the Egyptian and Syrian dynasties she sent out, she asserted a mental supremacy. But a nation that paid no homage to art or philosophy swallowed up all these dynasties, and with them all that remained of Greece herself. A universal polity was established, and the national life and family life of Rome perished at the very moment she established it.

Was there a religion connected with this universal polity, as with the family and the national? There was. The Emperor was the great God, to whom all peoples, nations and languages were to bow. Under this supreme divinity all others might be tolerated and recognised. No form of religion was to be proscribed unless absolutely incompatible with the worship of a Tiberius or a Vitellius. It has already been suggested that this Roman Empire exactly answers to the idea of a universal *world*. If there is to be something different, *a universal Church*, we ought to know what elements it is made of. We ought to know whether it too sets aside family and national life, or justifies their existence, reconciles them to itself, and interprets the problems of ancient history about their mysterious meaning.

### CHAPTER III. THE SCRIPTURAL VIEW OF THIS CONSTITUTION, I.

[Religious]{.smallcaps} people commonly acknowledge that the Bible is remarkably unsystematic. Sometimes they say so thankfully, even triumphantly, as proof that the Bible is meant to supply daily needs and meet the changing circumstances of the spiritual man. Sometimes it becomes an argument that others must do what the Bible does not do: those nearest the Apostles, or those at the Reformation, or those in a more advanced civilization. Sometimes it is a reason for denying that any book has the character Christians ascribe to this one. On that view the Bible is a collection of documents belonging to one nation, strung together by accident, and given a false wholeness by the superstition of later times.

All these views assume that *system* and *method* mean the same thing, so that a book without the first must lack the second. To me they are the greatest contraries. System is what is most opposed to life, freedom and variety, and method is what those things cannot exist without. To explain my meaning I would use an illustration rather than a definition, and the most striking one is the Bible itself. The systematizer is tormented on every page by the stubborn, hopeless material he has to handle. But the person who reads only for his own comfort and profit is, I am convinced, haunted by a sense of harmony. It lies not in the words but in the history, he ought not to overlook it, and without it the meaning of what he most loves is uncertain. This sense of method is also strengthened by the fact that these works were written at different periods, in different styles, by men of utterly different characters, for it makes the volume they form seem all the more marvellous. The most skilful analyst cannot persuade his disciples to give up the word *Bible*, and he cannot strip it of the feelings attached to it.

I\. It may help our purpose to look into this a little. Anyone reading the Old Testament sees that the idea of a covenant between God and a certain people governs it. In plain history, lofty prayers and songs, impassioned denunciations of evil and predictions of coming misery, this idea lies at the root. Take it away and the parts lose their connection, and each book, however simple, becomes an incoherent rhapsody. Someone who only wanted to understand that strange people, which has kept its identity through so many generations, would begin with the account of this covenant. He would see that the call of Abraham, the promise to him and his seed, and the seal given him are the most significant parts. But one thing would strike him above all: the covenant is with a *family*. It is with a man, but expressly as head of a family, and every promise is bound up with the hope of descendants. Abraham's patriarchal character cannot be treated as incidental to his being the chosen witness and servant of the Most High; the two are inseparable. His relation to God was interpreted to him through his human relations, and he could fulfil those relations because he acknowledged the higher one. Further reflection on the lesser parts of the narrative, which gain new meaning once this fact is seen as the centre, shows the following. The tribes among whom Abraham lived were sensual, and their worship was sensual. This involved neglect of family bonds.
