So the witness for an invisible, righteous God, against gods of nature and mere power, was by the same necessity the witness for the sacredness of those bonds. The *world* imagined a Being who exercised power over men, seen in the clouds and heard in the winds, and it trembled, until utter corruption brought utter atheism. Abraham, the beginner of the Church on earth, believed instead in a God *related* to men and made known through their human relations. But this truth could not be shown in one faithful individual; it had to be shown through a family. So the rest of Genesis gives the history of the patriarchs after Abraham. What if these, or some of them, were not faithful? What if they did not maintain family relationship, or remember the higher principle in it? What if the *world* should find its way into the Church? The historian does not wait to be asked; his narrative answers. Most of Jacob's sons were not faithful. They did not maintain family life or remember the Being who had revealed himself through it. Perhaps, then, Joseph, the true believer, separated himself from his godless brothers and founded a new, distinct fellowship? Had he done so, he would have acted on the principle of Ishmael or Esau, and founded a society built on choice, not on relationship. The historian says he did otherwise. He was separated from his brothers, but by their act, not his, and he remained a witness to God's covenant, not with himself, but with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob; not with an individual, but with a family. According to the Jewish Scriptures, then, the Abrahamic family was cut off by its covenant from the other families of the earth precisely so that it might witness for the true order of the world. Against that order all sensible idolatry and all independent choice or self-will is rebellion. In it alone men can be free, because to remain in it they must give up the inclinations that enslave them. And through it alone, as we might have guessed from the Gentile records, the idea of God can be imparted. The promise of the covenant was therefore that in the seed of Abraham all the families of the earth should be blessed.

### CHAPTER III. THE SCRIPTURAL VIEW OF THIS CONSTITUTION, II.

II\. Sentimentalists talk about the patriarchal condition of the world, its essential purity and the misery of leaving it, but the Scriptures give such dreams no support. It was part of the promise that Jacob's children would enter another state: they were to possess the Canaanite nations and become a nation. The history, true to all experience, describes the passage between the two conditions as grievous, for the children of Abraham sank into moral debasement and slavery. Yet their redemption is tied to a more awful revelation than any they had received, or, as far as we can see, could have received, in their earlier state. It leads to wonderful new discoveries about the relations between men and God. The God of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob declares that He remembers His covenant and has seen His people's affliction. But to the guide and deliverer He appointed, He declares Himself by another name: the name on which the Jewish covenant stands and which is the foundation of all law, I AM THAT I AM. The judgments on natural worship, and on a tyranny that scorned all invisible and righteous government, were accomplished, and the people learned that an unseen Power had delivered them. Then the awful code was proclaimed amid thunder and lightning. It spoke straight to each man's individual conscience, and it also reminded him, directly and solemnly, that he was related to God and to his brethren. I will not explain here how the tribal institutions, those of family relationship, were built into the Jewish constitution so that they gave this law its meaning without taking away its awful personal character. Anyone who studies the Pentateuch with the slightest attention must see it. It is more necessary to notice the institutions that lifted Jewish thoughts above family and law, though they were intertwined with both: the tabernacle, the priesthood and the sacrifices. Every thoughtful Jew knew these were shrines of an undeveloped mystery, and he was equally sure that the mystery was implied in all his acts, his family relations, the national order and his obedience to the law. The commandments declared that there was an awful, self-existent Being from whom all law came. The Tabernacle affirmed that He was present among His people and could be approached, in some awful manner. The family covenant witnessed to a relation between Him and His worshippers. The Priesthood, from generation to generation, witnessed that this relation could actually be realised, by the whole people in a representative. The national constitution and its punishments awakened in each person a sense of moral evil. They taught him that it arose from violating his relations with God and his countrymen, and that its effect was practical exclusion from those blessings. The sacrifices signified that the relation was restored when he had given something up, personally and through the priest. That something was not chosen by himself as most fitting or most precious, but appointed by the law, and with it he gave up the self-will that had caused the separation. Such thoughts were wrought gradually into every humble and obedient Jew. His great Lawgiver's parting instruction brought them home to him, and his later experience, and the teachers who showed him its purpose, confirmed and illustrated them.

The Jewish national polity was in its essence exclusive. We dwell on this as if it cut off all connexion between that polity and the Pagan or modern European ones. But every *nation*, as such, is exclusive. Athens was exclusive and so was Rome, yet all admit that more of humanity came out in them than ever appeared in the savage tribes that never reached a definite polity. Before we can tell whether Jewish exclusiveness was inhuman, we must find out what it excluded, and the answer is the same as before: it excluded the worship of sensible, natural things, and it excluded choice and self-will. The covenant with an invisible Being made it treason for men to choose their own objects of worship. Worship of the one Being was the bond of the commonwealth, and if it broke, the commonwealth dissolved. The covenant obliged them to see all kings as reigning by virtue of His covenant, representing His dignity and answerable to Him. It obliged them to see every other officer, priestly, prophetical or judicial, as receiving appointment and commission directly from Him. Its first protest affirmed that there is *not* a set of separate gods over each territory, varying with soil and climate, but one Almighty and Invisible Being who is Lord of all. The God of Israel is declared the God of all nations, and the Israelites are chosen out to witness to the fact. By the second protest the exclusive Hebrew witnessed that no king, priest or judge has a right to think himself possessed of intrinsic power. Each exercises an office under a righteous king, a perfect priest, an all-seeing judge. As he keeps that thought and fulfils his task in its strength, the character of that king, priest and judge, and his own relation to men, are revealed to him. And these offices continue from generation to generation as a witness to the permanence of Him who is Lord of them all and abides for ever.

As in the patriarchal period the Divine Being manifested Himself in family relations, and so showed what they depend on, how they are upheld and where their worth lies, so in the national period He was manifested through all national offices, and so explained their meaning, support and worth. But we must not suppose family relations had less to do with this stage than with the former. They were built into the national institutions, and those institutions depended on them. So their meaning, in connexion with national life, national sins and the Being to whom both witnessed, grew more apparent.

I need not point out to anyone who reads the prophets their constant method of awakening the Jew's conscience and giving him the highest truths. The Lord is always presented as the *husband* of the nation, and apostacy and false worship are constantly called adulteries. Great pains are taken to convince us that these are not poetical flourishes or terms of art, by connecting the actual human relation and offence with the spiritual one. The verbal commentator is often at fault because the two seem confused. He cannot decide whether the holy man is denouncing the nation's infidelity to her God or actual wives' infidelity to their husbands. Such perplexity is unavoidable for anyone determined to separate the two ideas, who does not see that the prophet's main object is to show how they bear on one another. They will not enter his mind and feel that human relationships are not artificial types of something divine. They are actually the means, and the only means, by which man rises to any knowledge of the divine. Every breach of a human relation implies a violation of the higher law and also hinders the perception of it, drawing a veil between a man's spirit and his God.

But how did this idea of a human constitution agree, or collide, with the attempts at *universal empire* that seemed the necessary end of the ancient polities? The Asiatic monarchies have sometimes been called *patriarchal*. Beyond doubt the patriarchal feeling, the belief that the king was a father, lay at their foundation and made up all that was sound in the monarch's acts or the people's reverence. But if we believe the Bible, the king is more than a father. His position is grounded in the acknowledgment of an unseen, absolute Being whose relations to men lead up to contemplating Him in Himself. The effort to make the paternal relation everything is therefore a false effort and leads to false results. Here the result is plain. The monarch's power has no safe ground, and soon is revered merely as power. No sense of a law they ought to obey is called forth in subjects or monarch. He may have kindly affections toward them, and they toward him, but that is all. Nothing preserves the existence and sanctity of the family relationship on which sovereign authority is built. Nothing resists the tendency to natural worship, which destroys it, and nothing prevents the monarch from believing he reigns by his own right. So these so-called patriarchal governments awaken neither the energies of the intellect nor the perception of right and wrong, and soon turn into the direct contraries of what they profess to be. The father becomes an oppressor of his own people and a conqueror of others, and all idea of the invisible is swallowed up in reverence for him. At last he is looked up to as God of gods and Lord of lords. It is no false feeling that makes us rejoice when these patriarchal kings were driven back by the little national bands at Marathon or Platæa. Anyone who reveres invisible more than visible strength will not hold back his pæans at their defeat. It is hateful and godless to check them, or to stir up sympathy for the Eastern tyrant. Whoever cherishes that habit of feeling will not really be able to rejoice, whatever he fancies, when Pharaoh and his host sink like lead into the waters, or when Sisera with his six hundred chariots is put to flight by the prophetess of Israel.

In the history of the Jews we find that their distinct polity, all the time it lasted, was a witness against these Babel monarchies. In them the Jew saw concentrated, in its worst form, the world that the covenant with Abraham's family and the Israelite nation had delivered him from. Yet his evil nature perpetually tended to be like this world, to share its splendours and adopt its worship, and at length he was punished by subjection to its tyranny. But the inclination was resisted by more than punishment. The wish for fellowship with other nations was a true wish inverted. The dream of a human polity was one the true God had sent the Jew, though an evil spirit had taught him how to realise it. The great office of the Hebrew Seer was to bring out the true idea of such a polity: to show how it lay hid in all their own institutions and how it would finally be fully manifested. Beside the vision of a Babylonian kingdom, which he taught his countrymen to see as based on a lying principle, the contrary of their own, and meant to scourge them if they took that principle into their conduct, rose another vision. It was of a king who did not judge by the sight of his eyes or the hearing of his ears, but ruled men in righteousness, and whom the heathen would own. Each new step in the covenant's history, the call of the patriarch that made them a family and the deliverance under Moses that made them a nation, was joined with a fresh revelation of the Divine King through these different relations, neither displacing the other but taking it into itself. So this glorious vision would have been utterly imperfect without the prospect of a discovery not granted to any former age. The prophet was trained in deep, awed meditation on the law, on his land's history, and above all on the mysterious services of the temple. As one sin and one judgment after another showed him his nation's dangers and wants, he came by degrees to see that the heir of David's throne must be a MAN, in as strict a sense as David was, capable of infinitely greater sympathy with every form of human sorrow. Yet in Him the worshipper must behold GOD, less limited by human conceptions and more in His own absoluteness and awfulness than even in the burning bush or amid the lightnings of Sinai. How could both longings be fulfilled? How could idolatry be abolished by the very manifestation that brought the object of worship nearer to human thoughts? How could belief in a Being nigh to men be reconciled with belief in one dwelling in His own perfection? How does unceasing action for His creatures agree with eternal rest? How could He be satisfied with men and yet incapable of satisfaction with anything less pure and holy than Himself? These were the awful questions that exercised the prophet's soul. They were answered not at once but in glimpses and flashes of light across the darkness of his own soul and his country's condition. Even now they startle us as we read, and make us feel that the words are meant to guide us through our own confusions, not to give us notions or formulas for disguising them. One part of his teaching must have come from the polity that was the great contrast to his own. The universal monarchs, the Sennacheribs and Nebuchadnezzars, were Men-gods. They took to themselves the attributes of the Invisible. In the same proportion as they hid from men's eyes anything beyond humanity, they became inhuman, cut off from their kind, dwelling apart in an infernal solitude.

This black ground brought the perfectly clear, bright object more distinctly into view. The prophets felt that the God-man, in whom the fulness and awfulness of Godhead would shine forth, might *therefore* have perfect sympathy with the poorest and most friendless. He might at the same time enable them to enter that transcendent region which their spirits had always sought and never been able to penetrate.

### CHAPTER III. THE SCRIPTURAL VIEW OF THIS CONSTITUTION, III.

III\. When we open the first book of the New Testament, its opening words say its subject is the Son of David and the Son of Abraham. As we read on, we find that the writer believes this person came into the world to establish a Kingdom. Every act and word recorded of Him refers to it. A voice in the wilderness cries that a kingdom is at hand. Jesus comes preaching the Gospel of the kingdom. He goes up a mountain to give the principles of His kingdom. Nearly every parable He tells to the people begins, "The kingdom of heaven is like." He heals the sick so that the Jews may know His kingdom has come near. His private talks with His disciples, as much as His public discourses, concern the character, establishment, and destiny of this kingdom. He is charged before Pontius Pilate with claiming to be a king, and the inscription on the cross proclaims Him one.

No one will deny that the Evangelists differ in character and style, or that a hundred theories may be offered about their origin, their agreements, and their differences. But it is equally true that this feature is common to all of them. The most sweeping theory of interpolation could not remove it, and discovering how the Gospels were formed would not touch it. The kingdom of Christ is, in one aspect or another, the subject of them all. It will be said that this is easily explained: the writers are Jews, and such language is peculiarly Jewish, the idiosyncrasy of the strangest and most bigoted people on earth. Up to a point this agrees with my own view. I have tried to show that the habit of thought behind this constant phrase is Jewish, and why. But it is a long step from that to the usual assumption that the phrase belongs only to particular accidents and circumstances and has nothing to do with what is essential and human. On my view, the Israelite was taken out of all nations to witness to what is permanent and not a matter of mere mode. He witnessed to the meaning of the relationships he shared with pagans and with us, which every pagan and every peasant among us feels to have a meaning. He witnessed also to the ground and purpose of national institutions and law, which the pagans acknowledged, and most of us acknowledge, as the great distinction between men and brutes. Pagans believed, and we believe, that beneath these relationships and this national order lies another order, not limited like the first and not exclusive like the second. So I cannot see why we should speak of the Jew's prejudices and idiosyncrasies because he states this universal idea in the simplest and aptest words. The Evangelists say that what was promised and expected is a Kingdom, and that Jesus of Nazareth is the King. Either claim may be denied: it may be said that men do not need a spiritual and universal society, or that this person lacks the credentials He claims. But by all ordinary rules of criticism we must admit that this was the Evangelists' idea, and in studying an author we should enter into his idea before substituting our own.

I know the objector has an answer, one that current religious opinion will support and many will weigh heavily. He will say we have an excuse for separating the inward sense of the Gospels from their Jewish accidents, in the inconsistency of the very phrases I cite. The Evangelists constantly present the kingdom as outward and visible, like David's and Solomon's, or that kingdom restored and extended, ready to replace Herod's government and perhaps in the end the Caesars'. Yet they also attribute to their Master such words as "The kingdom of God cometh not with observation," "The kingdom of God is within you," and "My kingdom is not of this world." These words show that He commonly taught another doctrine, which became mixed with coarser, more sensual elements. So, the objector says, are we not justified in breaking up the mass and taking out the pure ore?

I think the reader who has come with me this far will not be much shaken by this. The kingdom of David and of Solomon *was* distinguished from the kingdoms of this world, and it did not come with observation. It stood on a principle other kingdoms set at naught: that the visible king is the type of the invisible one, that he reigns by virtue of a covenant between the invisible king and the nation, and that he is subject to a divine law. All the great nations practically denied this principle, and denied it most emphatically when they became *kingdoms*, the ordinary and apparently necessary end of them all. The Israelite kingdom existed to enforce it. Throughout its history the nation and its kings tended to disregard the constitutional principle and forget the covenant, but that very tendency proved the truth of the idea it fought against. If so, what contradiction was there in saying that the new kingdom was the one promised to David, the kingdom of his son, and yet in the highest sense a kingdom the outward eye cannot observe, a kingdom within, a kingdom not of this world?

Do I mean that most or all who first heard these announcements found nothing startling in them? If I did, I would be rejecting the Evangelists' own testimony. They tell us that the leaders of the Jewish commonwealth, and all the popular sects that divided it, were continually perplexed and outraged by this language. But they also tell us that these same people had lost the family and national character of Hebrews. They perverted God's express commands about honouring father and mother. They felt no fellowship with Israelites as Israelites, and prided themselves on being different from their countrymen in righteousness or wisdom; individual self-exaltation of one kind or the other was their mark. In keeping with this, the Evangelists say these men were never so scandalised as when Jesus spoke of His *Father*, of coming to do His will, of knowing Him and being one with Him. The idea of a relation between men and their Maker, which the Abrahamic covenant implied, had wholly left them. So they could not cherish the hope of a full manifestation of the ground of that relation, the hope that had sustained every suffering Israelite in every age, the hope of Israel. Their idea of God was the heathen one: a Being in the clouds or spread through the universe, wholly separate from His worshippers, unable to speak through men to men, declaring Himself only by signs like the red sky of morning and the lowering sky of evening. So the king they expected was the counterpart of the absolute Emperor. The awful words "We have no king but Caesar" would not have been spoken at any other moment. It took intense hatred and all the passions then possessing their hearts to make the priests formally abandon the dream of Jewish supremacy, and they probably kept the right to hold one doctrine in the schools and another in the judgment-hall. Still, those words expressed the speakers' most inward thought: the king of Abraham's seed whom they wanted was a Caesar and nothing else.

But there were others who, amid much confusion and ignorance, really claimed their place as members of a nation in covenant with God. They had walked blameless in the Lord's ordinances, finding the deepest reality in every symbol of the Divine Presence that seemed a phantom to the world, and the merest phantoms in what the world called realities. They knew their own darkness but rested on the promise of a light that should rise on their land. They joined public shame to a miserable sense of moral evil and looked for a deliverer from both at once. The Roman soldier oppressed them not because he reminded them of tribute but because he told them the national life was gone, or lived only in their prayers. Under the fig-tree they had begged God to disperse the clouds hiding His face, to remember the poor, and not to let men have the upper hand. Whether or not they could reconcile in their understanding a kingdom ruling over all with a kingdom in their hearts, they inwardly acknowledged that only one fitting both descriptions could answer the cries they had sent to heaven. Whatever they saw of Him who was proclaimed king, and whatever they heard Him say, tended to bring these thoughts into harmony, or at least to make them feel each was necessary. He had power over the elements and over the secret workings of the human body. (I am of course assuming the Evangelists' story; my aim is to show that its parts are thoroughly consistent when viewed in light of one leading idea.) But He used this power for timid fishermen, paralytics, and lepers. He says His kingdom is like a grain of mustard-seed, the least of all seeds, which becomes a tree where the birds of the air lodge. He says also that the seed of the kingdom is scattered on different soils, and that the right soil is an honest *heart*. His *acts* had the most obvious outward effects, yet their main effect was to persuade the prepared observer that a communion had been opened between the visible and invisible worlds, and that the first was under the power of the second. His words were addressed to Israelites as children of the covenant, yet each tended to awaken in them a sense of *humanity*, a feeling that to be Israelites they must be more. All this general language prepared for the discoveries of the last supper, when, having loved His own who were in the world, He loved them to the end. There He announced that they were all united in Him as the branch is to the vine, that there was a still more wonderful union between Him and His Father which they might come to know through their union with Him, and that a Spirit would come to dwell with them and testify of Him and of the Father. All these discourses are gathered up in the amazing prayer, "That they all may be one, as thou, Father, art in me, and I in thee, that they may be one in us."

Either those words contain the essence and meaning of the whole history, or the history must be rejected as the wickedest lie and most awful blasphemy ever imposed on the world. And if they do contain its meaning, that meaning must be embodied in *acts*. So the Evangelists go on to record, in calm, simple words, their Master's death and resurrection. They are related as events, with no comment and few hints of effects to follow. The quiet, accurate detail makes us feel: "He certainly died, He whom we believed to be the Son of God and King of Israel; He actually rose in His body and came among us who knew Him, and spoke and ate with us. This is the accomplishment of the union between heaven and earth; it is no longer a word, it is a fact." The risen Lord tells His Apostles to go into the world and testify of this fact, and not only to testify but to adopt men into a society grounded on its accomplishment. Linked with that command, as the final basis of the universal society, a name is proclaimed in which the name revealed to Abraham and the more awful one Moses heard in the bush are combined and reconciled.

For someone who has looked at the Gospel merely as a case of great doctrines or fine moralities, the *Acts of the Apostles* must be utterly unintelligible. The specimens of apostolic preaching it gives refer comparatively little to our Lord's discourses or parables. They dwell mainly on the great acts of death and resurrection, as evidence that Jesus was the king, as explaining and completing the earlier history of the Jewish people, and as justifying and realising the truth working in the heathen mind, "that we are His offspring." But a person who really looks on the Bible as the history of the establishment of a universal, spiritual kingdom, the kingdom God always intended for men, of which the universal kingdom then existing in the world was the formal opposite, will find in this book exactly what the earlier records would be meaningless without.

One might expect the narrator of events as transcendent as the ascension of the Son of Man into the invisible glory, or the descent of the Spirit to take possession of the feelings, thoughts, and utterances of mortal men, to stand still and wonder at what he so fully believes. But he does not. He regards these events as the necessary consummation of all that went before and the necessary foundation of the Church's existence. So he can quietly relate other circumstances, however disproportionate they seem, that were needed for the Church's outward manifestation and growth, such as the Apostles meeting in the upper room and the completion of their number. If the foundation of this kingdom were the end of all God's purposes, the kingdom of God among men, its human conditions could be passed over no more than its divine ones. It was as needful to prove that the ladder had its foot on earth as that it had come down from heaven. As we go on, each step of the story leads us to see the Church as the child the Jewish polity had carried in its womb for so many ages. First its filial relation is shown: it is an Israelitic, not a *mundane*, commonwealth. Then it is shown that, though not mundane, it is essentially *human*, with a principle of expansion greater than the Roman empire's.

Here lies the apparent contradiction, and the real harmony, between the two aspects in which the Apostles of the Circumcision and St. Paul viewed this kingdom. The one witnessed to its continuity, the other to its freedom from all national exclusions; these, we may believe, were their respective offices. Yet each, in fulfilling his own, was teaching the other truth most effectively. St. Peter and St. James maintained the Church's universality while contending for its Jewish character and derivation. St. Paul maintained the national covenant while telling the Gentiles that if they were circumcised Christ would profit them nothing. Take away the first testimony and the Church becomes an earthly, not a spiritual, commonwealth, subject to earthly limits; take away the second and the promise to Abraham is unfulfilled. In another sense, as the canon of Scripture shows, St. Paul was more directly carrying out the spirit of the Jewish distinction by upholding separate ecclesiastical communities according to tribes and countries. And the Jerusalem Apostles were carrying out the idea of the Church's universality more than he did, by addressing its members as one whole community scattered through different parts of the world.

But we must remember that while this universal society, in the historical conception, grew out of the Jewish family and nation, in the theological conception it is the root of both. Aristotle says, "That which is first as cause is last in discovery." The letter to the Ephesians turns this formula into life and reality when St. Paul tells them they were created in Christ before all worlds, and says the transcendent economy was gradually revealed to the Apostles and Prophets by the Spirit. In this passage I think lies the key to the whole character of the dispensation and of the books that set it forth. Suppose the Gospel is the unveiling of a mystery hidden from ages and generations, and this mystery is the true constitution of humanity in Christ, so that a man believes and acts a lie who does not claim union with Christ. Then we can understand why the deepest New Testament writings are epistles rather than digests of doctrine. They explain to those already admitted into the Church their own position, bring out the side of it that bore on their circumstances or besetting sins, and show what life is consistent with it and what is at variance. We can understand why the first of these epistles, the one supposed most like a systematic treatise, opens by announcing a Gospel concerning Jesus Christ, who was made of the seed of David according to the flesh and marked out as the Son of God with power, according to the Spirit of holiness, by the resurrection of the dead. The fact of a union between Godhead and humanity is set forth as the one the Apostle was appointed to proclaim, the ground of the message to the Gentiles, and the implicit source of every idea of reconciliation, divine life, justification by faith, and sanctification by the Spirit. We can understand why his great fight with the Corinthians was over their raising certain notions, and certain men as representatives of them, into the place of the Lord of their fellowship, and why pride, sensuality, contempt of others, and abuse of ordinances followed necessarily from that sin. We can understand why he curses so vehemently the false teachers who denied the Galatians the right to call themselves children of God in Christ by the new covenant and sent them back to the old. We may see that his wonderful words to the Ephesians, that they sit in heavenly places in Christ Jesus, are as real and practical as the closing exhortations of the same letter on husbands and wives, fathers and children, and that the second are involved in the first. We may see the connection between his entreaty to the Colossians not to stoop to will-worship and the service of angels and his assertion that Christ is in them the hope of glory, the head in whom dwell all the riches of wisdom and knowledge. We may see how some of the Philippian Church could be enemies of the cross of Christ, their god their belly, their glory their shame, not because they had not been admitted as members of Christ but because they had not pressed on to realize their claim. We may enter a little into the idea of the Thessalonian letters, however we differ on the time or times of its accomplishment: that the mystery of iniquity and the mystery of godliness must be manifested together, and that the two kingdoms, always in conflict, are at certain great crises brought into open collision. We shall not need evidence of the Apostolic authorship of the epistle to the Hebrews to be convinced that it unfolds the relations between the national and the universal dispensation, between the shadow and the substance of a Divine humanity. It contrasts what let the worshipper expect perfect admission into God's presence with what actually admitted him, and what revealed God as the enemy of evil with what revealed Him as its conqueror. Nor is it inconsistent with what we have seen that its writer should present men's sin everywhere as unbelief in the blessings they are brought into at each stage of the Divine manifestation. With solemn earnestness, and with warnings of a fearful, hopeless apostacy, he urges his readers to believe that their position is the one all former ages aspired to, and to claim it. From these exhortations the transition is easy to the Catholic epistles, which some have found so hard to reconcile with St. Paul. And surely, if the faith that Romans and Hebrews press on men with such grand promises and dire threats were not faith in a living Being who adopted men into fellowship with Himself so that, *being* righteous by that union, they might *do* righteous acts, and so that, having claimed their place as members of a body, the Spirit might work in them to will and to do of His good pleasure, then the assertions that faith without works saves and that faith without works cannot save are hopelessly irreconcilable. But suppose the idea of St. Paul, as much as of St. James, is that faith has all its worth in so far as it unites us to an object and raises us out of ourselves, and no worth at all as a mere property in ourselves. If this is the very principle the whole Bible develops, one hardly sees what either position would be good for without the other. If our Lord came to bring men into a kingdom of righteousness, peace, and joy, grounded on fellowship with a righteous and perfect Being, then the notion that righteousness can ever belong to any man in himself, and the notion that everyone is not to show its fruits in himself, seem equally contradictory. Without this consideration, I believe, we shall be as puzzled by the sketch of a Christian man's life, discipline, and conflicts in the epistle of St. Peter, and by St. John's doctrine that love is the consummation of all God's revelations and all man's strivings, as by any earlier part of the book. For every verse of the first teaches that men do not gain a kingdom hereafter but are put in possession of it now, and that through chastisements and the oppositions of their evil nature they learn its character and enter its privileges. And the second affirms again and again in express words that love has been manifested to men, that they have been brought into fellowship with it so that by that fellowship they may rise to the enjoyment of it, and that this fellowship is for us as members of a family, so that he who loves God must love his brother also. With such thoughts in mind, I believe we may venture, hoping for the deepest instruction, on the study of the last book of the Bible. We may not be able to decide which of the chronological speculations about it is least untenable, or whether it speaks of the future or the past, or whether it describes a conflict of principles or of persons. But of this we shall have no doubt: at one period or through all periods it shows a real kingdom of heaven upon earth. Its principle must always be the same, and all kingdoms are meant to be subject to it. It maintains itself against an opposing tyranny whose ultimate law is brute force or unalloyed selfishness. And it must prevail, because it rests upon a *name* that expresses perfect Love and ineffable Unity, the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost.

### CHAPTER IV. SIGNS OF A SPIRITUAL SOCIETY.

[We]{.smallcaps} have seen the traces of a spiritual constitution for mankind. Its two parts, united by family ties and by locality, depend on a third part that is universal. A universal society can take two possible forms. One destroys the family and national principle; the other expands it. Scripture calls the first *this world* and the second *the church*. The principles of the world exist in the heart of every family and nation. They are simply men's natural tendencies and inclinations, and they are always threatening to become dominant. When they do, men are no longer recognized as related to a Being above them, or as sharing a common humanity. The Church is the witness for these facts, which it is natural for us to deny, so it must be a *distinct* body. If it loses its distinctness it loses its meaning, and, though the words may at first sound paradoxical, it loses its universality for all practical purposes. The question to examine, then, is this: are there any signs today of a spiritual and universal body on the earth? Do those signs identify it with the body Scripture speaks of? And are they an effective witness against the world?

#### Section I.—Baptism.

[For]{.smallcaps} the last 1800 years there has existed a rite called Baptism. It is not derived from the national customs of any of the peoples among whom it is found. Tribes of very different origin and character adopted it, and once they had received it they believed themselves members of a common society. That society was supposed to be connected with an invisible world, and with a certain worship and government. An immense proportion of the children of Europe are admitted to the rite very soon after birth, and it is generally performed by a particular class of officials. These are facts that need no proof. The only question is whether they have a meaning, and what it is.

The idea of the Scriptures, as far as we have traced it, is that Jesus Christ came to reveal a kingdom. That kingdom is founded on a union established in his person between man and God and between the visible and invisible worlds, and ultimately on a revelation of the divine Name. If the setting up of this kingdom, and the adoption of men into it, are not connected with baptism in the New Testament, then the facts just noted do not concern us, whatever their import. Even if our Lord Himself enjoined baptism, if He did so in terms that let us suppose it merely incidental to the purposes of His coming, we cannot prove that the universal society which acknowledges it now is the one He founded.

So let us see in the Gospels how far this is the case. One of the first events they announce is that John the Baptist came preaching in the wilderness of Judea, telling people to repent because the kingdom of heaven was at hand, and that Jerusalem and all Judea went out to him and were baptized in the Jordan, confessing their sins. The narrative is at least singular. Baptism is tied to a spiritual act (repentance), to a spiritual promise (remission), to the announcement of a kingdom, and to a hint that the kingdom would not be made up only of Abraham's children. Suppose the ceremony, as some imagine, was unknown until then: it was introduced at the very moment the kingdom began. Suppose, as others say, it was already used to receive Gentile converts into the outer court: then giving it to Jews signified that they were henceforth to stand on universal human ground. Either way this baptism was the preparation for the Gospel, though for all that appears so far it may have been only a preparation. But Jesus Himself goes down into the water, and as He comes out a voice from heaven proclaims Him the well-beloved Son, and the Spirit descends on Him in bodily shape. So the announcement that the Divine man, the king of men, had appeared was, according to the Gospels, connected with Baptism. They also treat it as the beginning of His public ministry and of all the acts attesting His descent from above. Yet this might have been needed only to mark out the leader, with no application to his disciples. But Jesus preached, "Repent, the kingdom of heaven is at hand"; He appointed Apostles to declare that kingdom; and these Apostles baptize. Even so, the nature of their message might mean that they were only continuing John's dispensation, and had nothing to do with that higher Baptism of the Holy Ghost and of fire which John said would supersede his own. Our Lord tells Nicodemus that he must be born again to see the kingdom of heaven. What is born of the flesh is flesh, and what is born of the Spirit is spirit; the fleshly man cannot understand even earthly things, much less these heavenly ones, which only He could reveal who had come down from heaven and was in heaven. This declaration of the transcendent character of the kingdom is joined to the words that unless a man is born of water and of the Spirit he cannot enter it. Yet perhaps even here the reference is to the spiritual eye in man, which this ordinance, like the earlier Jewish ordinances, might open, rather than to the actual gift of the promised Spirit; for it is expressly said that the Spirit was not yet given, because Jesus was not yet glorified. After He had risen, He appears to His disciples and says that all power is given Him in heaven and earth, and that they are therefore to preach the gospel to all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Ghost. This language is certainly strange, for it seems to look forward only to a spiritual kingdom. One piece of evidence is still wanting: after Christ's glorification and the descent of the Holy Ghost, did the Apostles still baptize with water? Peter stood among the disciples and told the Jews that God had made this Jesus, whom they crucified, both Lord and Christ; he told them to repent and be baptized and they would receive the gift of the Holy Ghost; and that same day three thousand were baptized. This may be enough to show that the writers of the Gospels and Acts believed baptism to be the sign of admission into Christ's spiritual and universal kingdom, and therefore that everyone receiving it was by that very fact a member. A son or servant of the Roman commonwealth, once freed, entered at once on the rights of a citizen, and all its immunities and responsibilities became his. In the same way the young Christian convert, taught from the Scriptures, could not doubt that from his baptism he was free of the brotherhood whose head was his Lord. However lofty the language describing that brotherhood, it described his state. If Christ came to make men sons of God, he was a son of God; if to make them members of His body, he was a member; if to endue them with His Spirit, that Spirit was given to him. His baptism said to him: this is your position, and you are to live according to its conditions. The life will not be easy but a perpetual conflict, a battle not with flesh and blood only but with principalities and powers, the rulers of spiritual wickedness in high places. But understand the battle. Your foes are not keeping you from obtaining a blessing; they are keeping you from enjoying one already obtained for you. They will laugh at you for pretending it is yours and tell you not to claim it. In the strength of this covenant you must claim it, or your life will be a lie. I ask anyone to read the Epistles calmly and say whether baptism could have borne any other sense for those who exhorted men, because they were baptized, to count themselves dead to sin and alive to God. The same holds for those who addressed men they mostly did not know personally, some known to be inconsistent and unholy, as being in Christ, elect, children of God; and for those who urged their disciples not to doubt that they had been admitted into the communion of saints, and warned that they would sink into apostasy if they did. But then I must also ask this. If Christ's kingdom was declared everlasting, and this sign was fixed from the first as the admission into it, and the sign still exists among us, why are we who have received it not in the same position, with the same privileges and responsibilities, as those who lived eighteen hundred years ago? Is Baptism not the sign of a spiritual and universal kingdom?

##### Objections.—1. *The Quaker*.

To this question various answers are given. I will consider first that of the Quakers.

I\. The Quakers find it utterly incredible and monstrous that a spiritual fact or operation should be shown by a visible sign. Their argument runs: "Either men are livingly united to the Divine Word, or they are not. If they are, the sign is useless; if they are not, it is false. If Christ's kingdom depends on outward ceremonies, how does it differ from the Jewish? What do the words mean, that John baptized with water but Christ with the Spirit and fire, if both baptisms are equally outward?"

These positions are so self-evident to the Quaker that Scripture is not allowed to contradict them. Quoting texts and commandments is useless, because these are primary truths that override every text and decide how it is read. If the Apostles acted against them, that only showed the Apostles were still ignorant and Jewish in outlook. Be it so. If these notions are good for anything, and do not contradict the leading positive truths of Quakerism, let them be upheld at all risks. But that is the point I want to examine.

We have seen that Fox did not think it the work of the *Gospel* to reveal the fact of men's relation to the Divine Word. He believed that fact was made known to Heathens and to Jews alike, and to the Jews by a sign. By this means the Invisible Teacher told the children of Abraham that there was a union between them and Him. He warned them of the tendency of their fleshly natures to separate from Him, and promised to uphold them against it.

Fox acknowledged this to be the Divine method in the Jewish dispensation. He never claimed that the union made known to the Jew was a *material* union, for then there would have been nothing to signify, and so no sign. Nor did he claim it was a *variable* union, depending on the feelings of the people sharing in it, for then the thing signified would change every day, and again there could be no sign. So the fitness of this method, its very possibility, came from the fact that a spiritual and permanent relation was being made known by it. Yet the Quaker's only reason for abandoning the method is that the Gospel dispensation is spiritual and permanent, not material and transitory! This inconsistency needs a better justification than vague declamation against carnal practices, and angry denunciation of all Christendom, from the Apostles downward, for committing them.

"But it is false to give the sign to anyone who lacks the reality." What does *lacks the reality* mean? Does it mean the relation is not real? Then Fox was wrong, for he affirmed it *was* real, for all men. Or does "real" refer to the man's feeling and acknowledgment of the relation? Then the proposition says it is false to tell a man a truth because he does not believe it. We are certainly guilty of that falsehood, and so was the whole Old Testament dispensation, and so are Fox and the Quakers themselves.

"But the sign is useless to a man truly united to the Divine Word." This implies two opinions, both important and both illustrating Quaker feeling and history. One is that it is nothing to a man that a thing is true in itself and universally, so long as he feels it to be true for him. The other is that union with the Divine Word is all men need. Every earnest word of Fox testified against both. First, as I have shown often, the truth of the thing was the ground on which he urged men to base their feeling of it. Second, he declared that union with the Divine Word did not satisfy the Heathens or Jews who perceived it, but made them long for something more, a kingdom of Heaven. Here is evidence for Baptism that all the history of Christendom could not have given, freely offered by those who reject it. Their whole preaching is against Judaism and the old covenant, yet they are thrown back on Judaism and cannot rise above its great doctrine. And they cannot keep even that doctrine. They cannot keep the faith that we are related to the Divine Word, and can only put in its place certain individual feelings and impressions.

With this in mind, let us compare for a moment our reading of John the Baptist's words with theirs. We say John came baptizing with water unto repentance, for the remission of sins; that was the spiritual meaning of *his* Baptism. Our Lord's baptism, we hold, includes that meaning but has a deeper one. It does not merely show that the heart has turned to God and so been delivered from sin. It gives the spirit and power from which repentance and every right act must flow, and it brings its subject under the discipline of the purifying fire that is to consume the old and evil nature. This reading seems literal enough, and it agrees exactly with the promises and anticipations of the prophets and the expositions and retrospections of the Apostles. How does the Quaker improve on it? *He* makes it the great mark of John that he baptized with water, and of Christ that He would not. So the voice crying in the wilderness said: "Hear, O Israel! Rejoice, O ye Gentiles! The glorious time is at hand which your fathers expected and the whole universe has groaned for: the time when signs are to be abolished. The great Prince and Deliverer is at hand, who will make the things of earth no longer pledges and sacraments of a union with Heaven! *This* is the consummation of all mankind's hopes, *this* is what is meant by the Tabernacle of God being with men, by His dwelling with them, and their being His people, and His being their God."

##### 2. *The Anti-pædobaptist*.

II\. The Anti-pædobaptist (one who rejects infant baptism) is in many ways the opposite of the Quaker. He values the baptismal sign very highly and believes it is meant to witness to a spiritual kingdom. He generally holds firmly, as the Quaker does not, that God chooses men for their place in the Divine Economy. But he thinks that admitting people with no spiritual consciousness or capacity destroys the ordinance's meaning. As it exists in modern Europe, he says, it has nothing to do with the Kingdom of Christ.

I would answer this objection with great care, since it touches my whole argument, if it had not already been so fully considered. The question between us and the Anabaptists is not whether a sign points to a kingdom, but what kingdom it should point to. I have maintained, on the authority of Scripture, that the Catholic Church is a kingdom for *mankind*, grounded on the union between God and man established in Christ. I have maintained that it grew out of a family and a nation, and proved itself the proper and only foundation of both. If this notion is false, it may be reasonable to treat a child, an embryo man, as no citizen of this kingdom. To anyone who believes it true, such a doctrine must seem monstrous. Take a member of either class from which the early Church was formed, first a Heathen. The sins of the Roman empire have alarmed him with coming judgment: the divorces, adulteries, incests and parricides of its leading men. He has seen how little his countrymen's idea of the gods restrained such atrocities. A Christian Apostle's preaching has shown him that the evil nature behind all these crimes is in himself. He hears of deliverance from it. God has revealed himself as the enemy of all unrighteousness and also as men's Father. His Son has come to dwell among men, made himself the brother of our race, claimed its members as members of his body, given them a sign of admission, and promised them his Spirit. Could the man who received this joyful message and obeyed it doubt that he had been received into the true human family, out of a hateful, anomalous, inhuman world? Could he then say, "This child I have begotten belongs to that world. He has a human form and face, the form and face Christ bore, yet the accursed nature I have renounced is his rightful master, the evil society I have fled is his home, and I leave him to the evil spirit who, I believe, has spread his leaven through that nature and society"? I am not reasoning now with someone who thinks little of Baptism, but with one who believes it truly the sign of the redeemed covenant family. I ask him what less than this a Christian convert could understand from being told not to baptize his child because he could not be sure the child was included in Christ's redemption.

This is not the whole contradiction in such a prohibition. The Gospel reveals truths that are expressed in the forms of family life and seem expressible in no other. The convert reached them through feelings awakened by his home relations, or by his awareness of having violated them. All links between human relations and Divine would seem to him destroyed if he could not speak of his child as joined to him in a spiritual bond. And the Gospel promises a Spirit who awakens all consciousness, conviction and affection. That promise is equally trifled with by the doctrine that these must already exist before anyone can be admitted to the Gospel Covenant.

On this last point the perplexity of a Hebrew Christian told not to baptize his child must have been still worse. His own covenant had been emphatically with children. What replaced it was in every other respect wider and freer, referring every act of the creature more directly to the love and good pleasure of the Creator. Yet Christ is not recorded as saying a single word to this effect: *I command you not to follow the analogy of God's earlier dispensation, and not to suppose that in my kingdom of grace infants count as human and moral beings, as they did under the law*. Many recorded acts and words point the opposite way, that infants were a most honoured part of the race he came to seek and save. The convert confesses daily that the Redeemer of humanity entered childhood as well as manhood. Yet he must give up all the habits of thought and feeling God himself had taught him, not because they were too narrow, but because they were too comprehensive for his new position.

So the Anti-pædobaptist cannot plead, and this is his only plea, that baptizing infants is a strange departure from the admitted sense and purpose of the ordinance. I rather suspect the danger lies on the other side. It was so reasonable and inevitable a consequence of the baptismal principle that infants should be received into the Church, and this so clearly explained the Church's propagation and reconciled it with God's ordinary providence, that it is no wonder another equally necessary truth was lost sight of in the eagerness to press the practice. It might be forgotten that we baptize children not because they are children but because they are embryo men; that a spiritual blessing needs a receiver as well as a giver; and that Baptism is not a momentary act but a perpetual sacrament. Before I finish this section I may show that some or all of these errors have arisen in the Church, and the rise of a sect of Anti-pædobaptists is no doubt to be attributed to their prevalence. But in every country where Baptism is practiced there stands beside it an institution that bears far more complete witness against such perversions than those who reject the principle itself have been able to bear. This institution does not displace Baptism but *confirms*, as its name says, the authority and pledges of that sacrament. It declares to the child that the One who has guided it through infancy will be with it in the conscious struggles of manhood, and that it has been made free not only of one congregation but of the Universal Church.

The Anabaptist doctrine, then, like the Quaker's, strongly supports my position. It shows that the more Baptism's operation is restricted, the more belief in a human society becomes impossible.

Some will say the Baptism now found in Christendom is invalid because it is usually done by sprinkling rather than immersion. Since this notion is accidentally connected with the Anti-pædobaptist theory, I add a few words on it here. "The practice of the early ages, so far as we can tell, was to immerse. Sprinkling destroys Baptism's emblematic character as a burial. And if we admit an outward ceremony at all, we cannot then pretend the mode of performing it is indifferent." There is truth in each of these propositions. I admit, with probably most Churchmen, that early times very probably practiced what is least likely to have been introduced later, and that most known facts confirm this. I admit that "buried with him" in the sixth chapter of Romans is a better argument for immersion than St. Peter's "sprinkled from an evil conscience" can ever be for the modern custom. And I admit that having received one form and not another as the sign of a certain thing, we have no business making light of the particulars prescribed. But here is the distinction. Our Lord *prescribed* no particular mode of baptism. He said that water shall be the sign, used with certain words. He did not say, Use it thus and in no other way. However high the probability that a present practice differs from an earlier one, it does not, I contend, become illegitimate if it answers to the terms of the law that ordained it. This applies especially to a Universal Institution. We cannot tell that the old practice, however right, was not shaped by the circumstances of a particular country or time, or that the liberty to alter the mode was not contemplated in the terms of the enactment. A sign divinely instituted for mankind is too serious a thing to be settled by guesses about antiquity. These may be most useful as suggestions and may recover meanings we have lost, but they cannot condemn what the One who appointed the sign has not condemned. Least of all may we suppose that a sign is not big enough or does not involve enough self-sacrifice. That sets aside the first principle on which every sign's validity rests: the one the Ruler has fixed, great or little, is the right one and must denote what he meant it to denote.

I hope this is plain. If not, the ring in marriage may illustrate it. If a law prescribes a ring for marriages, *a* ring is indispensable. But the strongest evidence that wedding rings had commonly been gold, and the absence of any evidence that they had been anything else, should not void a marriage concluded with some other ring. This also bears on the alleged loss of the baptismal *emblem* through sprinkling. Suppose someone impeached a marriage celebrated with a brass or copper ring, arguing that the nobler metal implied purity, and even proved that this notion had attached to it and given rise to the custom. A judge would not listen for an instant, though he would grant most strongly that marriage implies purity and that all marriage institutions aim to preserve it. He would declare at once that the *emblem*, however interesting, useful for personal meditation, or lawful as the basis of a custom, is altogether distinct from the *sign*. The prescribed sign testified that the union was complete and final. A purely arbitrary sign with no emblematic value would do this, and its force as arbitrary and appointed must not be lost through considerations of wisdom or propriety. This is especially needful here. A divinely appointed institution will carry profound wisdom and many adaptations to the creatures it is meant for. A symbol like water suggests numerous hints and analogies, and more come to an earnest thinker, even one not fanciful. But the main fact Baptism witnesses to, our adoption into Christ, must be the key to these analogies, not they the key to it. That adoption must be received as a fact on the authority of the sign, without regard to any apparent likeness between sign and thing signified. Otherwise the sign loses its universality and counts for nothing to those who, through a weak faculty of comparison or any other cause, cannot see the resemblance.

These remarks only aim to show that nothing warrants the notion that our present Baptism is not formally the baptism Christ ordained, and so not the sign of admission into his universal and spiritual kingdom, merely because its mode may differ from the Apostles' practice. Whether any power, and what power, has the right to prescribe a mode of Baptism for the whole Church or any branch of it is a quite separate question, to be considered in its proper place.

##### 3. *The Modern Protestant*.

III\. Next to the Anabaptist comes the self-styled disciple of Luther and Calvin, the modern Protestant or Evangelical. His doctrine is that Christ has two kingdoms, one real and spiritual, the other outward and visible. It is highly desirable, perhaps necessary, that the young as well as the old be admitted into the outward one, and Baptism is the appointed way in. What privileges belong to this "Gentile court" they do not precisely settle. Perhaps some grace is given in Baptism. If not, there is great benefit in being allowed to hear preaching and receive a Christian education, which may be put to greater use later. But the important thing is to press on men that until they are actually and consciously converted they are not members of Christ or children of God. Some in this school believe these words may be applied to baptized people "in a sense." Ask in what sense, however, and the answers are so vague that they leave a painful impression. The language is awful and significant, yet it seems it may on occasion be played with, or used with a secret reservation.

But those who say this answer that they want to get rid of equivocations, not invent them. They resort to the hypothesis of a double kingdom because plain observation shows that a baptized man may be very evil. Being evil, he seems to have nothing to do with a kingdom of righteousness, peace, and joy in the Holy Ghost. They also say: "Let people devise whatever theological scheme they please, we know that we, baptized in infancy, passed in manhood from death to life as truly as any heathen in the first age could have done." It is neither fair nor honest, they contend, to suppress either fact: the one is obvious to every man's common sense, the other they know in their own consciousness. Yet it is suppressed, and must be, if we take Baptism to be what it is sometimes called, a New Birth, the actual introduction into a spiritual world.

I am as unwilling to deny the melancholy truth that Christian men are not living Christian lives as the Jewish prophet was to pass over the fact that God's name was blasphemed through his countrymen in all lands. I believe the precedents of Scripture apply strictly to us. The preacher's function now is what it was then: as the prophet in Jerusalem rebuked sin and called men to repentance, so must we in London or Paris. The question is what sin we are to rebuke and what repentance we are to call for. The Jewish prophet charged his people with forgetting the covenant of their God, and he traced all their sins to that one. The Jew was guilty because he did not claim a Jew's privileges and did not act as a Jew. Are we to follow this precedent or not? Do we follow it when we say, "This covenant is, I will be to you a father, and you shall be to me sons and daughters; you are acting as if you were not in it, you are forgetting it"? Or do we follow it when we say, "These titles are not yours, or are yours only in some formal imaginary sense," which in plain English means in no sense at all?

I am just as unwilling to deny or explain away the other assertion, that baptized men who have lived without God in the world are converted to him by his grace. I believe St. Bernard, Tauler, and à Kempis held this as strongly as any modern Methodist, and I might add Loyola and Xavier. They did not confine it to open profligacy, though they did not exclude that. They applied it to laymen and priests who, under a respectable exterior, had sought the praise of men more than the praise of God. Whether we may restrict the word "conversion" to a particular act or crisis is a matter for serious reflection. So is whether every act of repentance is not a conversion, a turning to God, and whether we forget that each such act is as much the work of God's Spirit as recovery from habitual thoughtlessness and sin. But however these are decided, they do not affect the opinion that someone who has received Christian Baptism may undergo an entire change of feelings and aims. By what words, then, is such a revolution to be described? I believe the answer comes from comparing approved records of conversions, which differ greatly. In some, a man speaks with horror and loathing of his past years and his youthful companions. Another turns that loathing against his own evil nature and himself for yielding to it. He shows the deepest affection for all he has known, owns them more righteous than himself, believes God cares for them as for him, and is certain that what is true for him is true for them. The first talks much of his new start, his new heart, his purified affections. The second rejoices that, having found his own heart feeble, he has been led to see another in whom he ought to have trusted before and may trust now. The first speaks of the grace bestowed on himself; the second of being taken under the guidance of a Spirit he has resisted too long. These ways of speaking may be intermingled, there may be true feeling in those who mostly use the first, and there may be errors and confusions in records where the second predominates. Yet does not everyone recognize a characteristic and most practical difference between them? Would not any experienced Evangelical feel that the one language shows a healthier, more genuine character than the other? Then ought he not to ask whether both kinds of language are incompatible with Baptismal Regeneration, or only one, and if only one, whether the false or the true? Suppose "then I was awakened" does not imply "I had been asleep." Suppose "then I came to the knowledge of the truth" does not imply that what I knew was true before I knew it. Suppose "I ceased to strive against the Spirit" does not imply earlier resistance to the Spirit. Then these are mere cant words, good for nothing and utterly detestable. But if they do imply all this, they imply just what the believer in Baptismal Regeneration is charged with fiction and falsehood for maintaining. They presume a state that is our state, whether we are conscious of it or in conformity with it or not.

So it is not necessary, to vindicate these two facts, to adopt the notion of two kingdoms, one earthly, formal, and fictitious, the other heavenly, spiritual, and real. Neither fact can be calmly examined, even in the reports of those who insist on them most, without suggesting that there is a heavenly, spiritual, real kingdom. All evil men, just so far as they are evil, are rebelling against it, and all converted men, just so far as they are converted, are brought into subjection to it. Therefore whatever evils have flowed and are flowing from the notion of two kingdoms are not justified or offset by a single practical advantage. How practical those evils are, let the history of Christian Europe since the Reformation attest. I have spoken of the difference between Luther and the Lutherans, and between Calvin and the Calvinists. I have spoken of how Justification by Faith has been turned from a living principle into an empty shibboleth, and how Divine Election has lost its force except as an excuse for doubting our own awful responsibilities. If we trace these miserable fruits to their root, I believe we shall find it in this notion. This at least is certain, as I have had occasion to remark again and again: Luther did not hold Baptismal Regeneration alongside Justification by Faith, as he might have held some doctrine belonging to the natural philosophy of his age. He *grounded the one on the other*. "Believe on the warrant of your Baptism. You are grafted into Christ; claim your position. You have the Spirit, you are children of God; do not live as if you belonged to the devil." This was his invariable language, and with it he shook the Seven Hills.

What, I ask, have those done who have abandoned this language? While they talk of Luther, they would denounce as heretical and papistical anyone who used it. The children of Protestant families are told they have no right to call themselves children of God. They grow up in that conviction, and in maturer years they carry it to its logical consequences. They feel they have no right to use the Lord's Prayer, or to pray at all. They feel there is no power near them to keep them from temptation, and no bond of fellowship with anyone except liking and taste. As their understandings ripen and their feelings decay, they come to regard Protestantism as a halfway house between Popery and Infidelity. Whether they go back to one or on to the other depends mainly on circumstances, and on whether fancy or intellect prevails in their constitution. I speak of the more courageous; in the majority, dull indifference, incapable of either resolve, becomes the ruling habit of mind. Thanks be to God, the exceptions in all Protestant countries are innumerable. But I believe they will almost universally be found where parents acted on the principle I am maintaining while disavowing it in words. They treated their children as if they possessed all Christian titles and privileges, in utter disregard of their own theory. I can say with some confidence that even in such cases the contradiction has not been harmless. A sense of perplexity and half-sincerity clings to the minds of those who most long to keep a clear heart and a free conscience. They do not dare to call themselves by a name which they feel they must claim if they are to serve God or do any right act. So their conduct becomes uncertain and their thoughts unmanly, and in place of humility they cultivate a false shame, in which they are conscious that pride is a large ingredient. There are hundreds of young men who will understand my meaning, and there are others, ministers of the Gospel, to whom I wish I could make it intelligible. At all events, those who feel as I do will have bitter cause for self-reproach if they do not protest, in season and out of season, against a notion which, unless I am greatly mistaken, is doing more than any other to undermine the Christianity of the Protestant nations.

##### 4. *The Philosopher*.

IV\. Last come our modern Philosophers. Their view of Baptism is usually put something like this. Baptism cannot be the sign of a universal society, because it shuts out Pagans and Mahometans, everyone but the members of one religious sect. It cannot properly be the sign of a spiritual society, because it makes no distinction between the stupidest and the most cultivated, the most brutal and the most humane. By its very words it implies acceptance of a doctrine that many Christians deny, few think about, and none understand. Judging by the words and ceremonies that everywhere go with it, it presumes belief in an evil spiritual agent, a belief that belongs only to the darkest ages. Baptism was certainly a bond of fellowship in certain periods, and it meant something to those who lived then. But its significance is gone and it has become a worthless symbol. It may be tolerated so long as it claims to be nothing, but the moment it tries to recover its obsolete importance, wise men will reject it altogether.

To the first objection, that Baptism is exclusive because it is only for those who profess faith in Christ, I reply as follows. Against theologians who regard Christ merely as the great teacher, the argument has great force. If they are right, to baptize men into Christ's name is to receive them into the school of a man who appeared in Palestine 1800 years ago. We may prefer him to one who appeared in Arabia about 1200 years ago, but our taste, which more information may change entirely, is no true foundation for a human fellowship. But this is not the idea of Baptism expressed in any formulary recognised anywhere in Christendom. That idea assumes that Christ is the Lord of men and that men are created in him. This is the constitution of our race, and so every attempt of men to break themselves into separate units is contradictory and doomed to fail. Say, if you like, that this view is utterly false, that it does not explain how men are related or what their history means, that mankind has no spiritual constitution, or that it cannot be known, or that it is not this. But you cannot say that, if it *were* this, a society founded on it would be a party society and not a society for mankind. By our doctrine we must tell Jews, Pagans and Turks that there is a fellowship for them as well as for us, and that we have no spiritual privilege to which they have not an equally indefeasible right. We protest against the Jews because they deny this and maintain that there is no fellowship for mankind. We protest against the Pagans because, by giving us different objects of worship, they divide us by circumstances, customs and places. We protest against the Mahometans because, by affirming the greatest man to be merely a man, they destroy the communication between our race and its Maker. They suppose that communication to exist, if at all, only for certain sages and not for every human creature. They set absoluteness against relationship, when each is involved in and depends on the other. So they make it *impossible* for Islamite nations to have any feeling of a common humanity, or to be anything but slaves.

Next it is said that our baptismal fellowship is not spiritual, because it takes no account of spiritual differences among men: the dullest clod has the same place in it as the man who sees furthest into the meaning and life of things. Here again I must recall the objector to the baptismal principle. He may think we are using a mere form of words when we say that a man or child is actually adopted into union with a Being above himself, and that the Spirit of Life, Power and Wisdom is given to him. It may seem to him sheer absurdity. We do not think it so. And if it were a truth, the accusation would have no ground. We would then be bearing witness that the great distinction of all is between the man who has an eye for spiritual things and the man who sees only the ground at his feet. And we would be doing more: taking men into a position where they might, if they would, overcome their downward tendencies and reach the highest insight. Certainly we do not look for intellectual or moral aptitude and *expose* the children in whom we do not find it. We believe there is an eye in all men that can be opened if the evil will does not keep it shut, that all special faculties are subordinate to this, and that they will be best awakened when it is most exercised. Philosophers of this day cannot object to that doctrine *on principle*, however little they may habitually act on it.

The third complaint is that this universal sign is inseparably tied to belief in an incomprehensible dogma. When we examined the Unitarian creed, we noticed as its most striking feature the assertion that the unity of God is a great, primary, unchangeable truth on which all others rest, and whose acknowledgment must be the foundation of unity among men. This seemed to us of the highest importance, and we said that no theory or dogma, whatever its authority or arguments, could destroy its force. But we found that the so-called Unitarians had turned this awful, everlasting truth into a mere notion. It was a notion deduced from material considerations and so self-contradictory. It was purely negative, telling Polytheists "*You ought not to worship many gods*" without declaring the one God they ought to worship. It led not to the adoration of a Living Being but to a superstitious reverence for the number one, and so it could never be the symbol of a human fellowship. If this unity is at the root of all union among men, the foundation on which the pillars of the universe rest, then we should look not for a crude notional announcement of it but for a gradual discovery of it through the forms and relations of human society. I have urged that such a discovery is found in the Scriptures. The name revealed to the father of the faithful was its first step, "Hear, O Israel, the Lord thy God is one Lord" the second, and the name into which we are baptized the final and perfect step. I do not say this is a progress from the obscure to the intelligible, or from the remote to the near. Like all science, it advances from what the Senses or Affections can apprehend to what is deeper and within reach only of the Understanding or Conscience. It then goes on to those amazing abysses which the Reason seeks, delights in, wonders at, and is lost in. Otherwise it would not be a revelation of God. As it is, we believe it is not the cold denial of everything men have dreamed in all ages, but the sweet reconciliation and harmony of all past thoughts, anticipations and revelations. No Pagan mythology could exist without acknowledging a *Something* behind all its conceptions of the gods, too awful to speak of, almost to think of. Each mythology also had its *Heroes* of divine and human race, whom men could admire and sympathise with. And even that was not enough without the dream of an *Inspirer, Life-giver*, not removed from men, not merely an object to behold and adore, but the source of their deeper thoughts and longings. I need not tell how dark and sensualized this faith became: the absolute Being seen as a dreary fate, the Heroes as products of earthly passions and full of earthly crimes, the Inspirer as the god of folly and drunkenness. Nor need I tell how these beliefs jarred even at their best, always seeking a unity and not finding it. Still, the feelings were really there. Poets and sages expressed them, but only because it was their gift to utter what was in the hearts of poor men, who were obscurely feeling or dimly acting it out. Now suppose the Name into which we Europeans have been baptized for so many centuries is, as we believe, what brings all these thoughts together, separates them from their hateful additions, raises them to Heaven, and yet ties them more directly to the daily business of this earth. Are we then guilty of fencing men off from our Communion by a strange dogma they can know nothing of, when we tell them that this Name is to go with them from cradle to grave? Are we guilty when we say that its grace, love and fellowship will be with them as a charm against every peril, a light in darkness, a comfort in sorrow, binding them to what was in the beginning, is now, and ever shall be?
